# 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre

"June 4th movement" redirects here; not to be confused with [May Fourth Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_Fourth_Movement).

"Tiananmen Square protests" redirects here. For other uses, see [Tiananmen Square protests (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiananmen_Square_protests_\(disambiguation\)).

For the military crackdown specifically, see [People's Liberation Army at the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_at_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre).

| Casualties |  |

[Protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests) led by students and workers, known in [China](5405.md) as the **June Fourth Incident**,[^cite_note-4] were held in [Tiananmen Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiananmen_Square) in [Beijing](18603746.md), China, from 15 April to 4 June 1989. After weeks of unsuccessful attempts between the [demonstrators](266137.md) and the [Chinese government](23239.md) to find a peaceful resolution, the Chinese government initiated martial law in late May and [deployed troops to occupy the square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_at_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre) on the night of 3 June in what is referred to as the **Tiananmen Square massacre**. The events are sometimes called th&#x65;**&#x20;'89 Democracy Movement**,[^cite_note-5] the **Tiananmen Square Incident**,[^cite_note-6] or the **Tiananmen uprising**.[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-usnews-8] The Chinese government terms the events the **political turmoil between the spring and summer of 1989**.[^cite_note-9]

The protests were initiated by [the death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Hu_Yaobang) of former pro-reform [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP) [general secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) [Hu Yaobang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Yaobang) in April 1989, amid the backdrop of rapid economic development and social change in [post-Mao China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China_\(1976%E2%80%9389\)). The movement's goals reflected anxieties among the people and political elite about the nation's future. Common grievances at the time included [inflation](38286.md), [corruption](3608404.md), dismantling of the "[iron rice bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_rice_bowl)", limited preparedness of graduates for the new economy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998216-10] and restrictions on political participation. Although the protests were highly disorganised with varying goals, student protestors called for reforms including the rollback of the removal of iron rice bowl jobs, greater political accountability, constitutional due process, [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy), [freedom of the press](105323.md), and [freedom of speech](21401843.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a34–35-11][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2001-12] Workers' protests were generally focused on economic issues, specifically inflation and Deng's economic reforms, which weakened Mao-era guarantees such as lifetime employment in [state-owned enterprises](204768.md) and associated workplace benefits.[^cite_note-Lin-2006-13] These groups united around anti-corruption demands, adjusting economic policies, and protecting social security.[^cite_note-Lin-2006-13] At the height of the protests on May 17, about one million people were assembled in the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001171-14]

As the protests developed, Chinese authorities responded with both conciliatory and hardline tactics, exposing deep divisions within the party leadership.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaich1990172-15] By May, a student-led [hunger strike](222292.md) galvanised support around the country for the demonstrators, and the protests spread to some 400 cities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas2006-16]

On 20 May, the [State Council](44498.md) declared [martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_from_the_State_Council_on_the_Imposition_of_Martial_Law_in_Parts_of_Beijing), and as many as 300,000 troops were mobilized to Beijing, but were halted by mass nonviolent resistance from the protestors and Beijing's civilian population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas2006-16][^cite_note-17][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiles2009-18][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeclassified_British_cable-19] After several weeks of standoffs and confrontations between the army and demonstrators, a 1 June meeting among the CCP's leadership decided to clear the square.[^cite_note-papers-20][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiles2009-18][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeclassified_British_cable-19] Chinese troops advanced into central parts of Beijing through the city's major highways in the early morning hours of 4 June, and engaged in bloody clashes with demonstrators attempting to block them, in which many people – demonstrators, bystanders, and soldiers – were killed or injured. The vast majority of killings were clustered in Beijing's western suburbs along [Chang'an Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'an_Avenue). Estimates of the death toll vary from several hundreds to the low thousands, with thousands more wounded.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHow_Many_Died1990-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Sino-American_relations''1991445-22][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998154-23][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristof:_Reassessing_Casualties-24][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERichelsonEvans1999-25][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalls_for_Justice2004-26] On June 5, after military forces cleared the Square, an unidentified individual, later referred to as "[Tank Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tank_Man)" briefly stood in front of a column of tanks, temporarily blocking their movement. The photograph of the event became widely circulated internationally and is often regarded as one of the [most recognizable images of the 20th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_photographs_considered_the_most_important). It is subject to [censorship in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_China).[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29]

The event had both immediate and longer-term consequences. Western countries imposed arm embargoes on China[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDube2014-30] and various Western media outlets labeled the crackdown a [massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre).[^cite_note-b-31][^cite_note-npr-32] In the aftermath of the protests, the Chinese government suppressed [other protests around China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Chinese_protests_by_region), carried out mass arrests of protesters[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiles199728-33] which catalysed [Operation Yellowbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Yellowbird), strictly controlled coverage of the events in the [domestic](#Official_narrative) and [foreign affiliated press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_media_at_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre), and [demoted or purged officials it deemed sympathetic to the protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_purges_during_and_after_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre). The government also invested heavily into creating more effective police [riot control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riot_control) units. More broadly, the suppression was followed by a halt to [political reforms which began in 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China#Political_reforms) as well as the [New Enlightenment movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Enlightenment_\(China\)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWu2015-34][^cite_note-Pei-2019-35] and a slowdown in liberalising policies of the 1980s, which were later partly resumed after [Deng Xiaoping's southern tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping's_southern_tour) in 1992.[^cite_note-Brown-2009-36][^cite_note-37][^cite_note-\:1-38] In the longer term, political liberalisation was curtailed, while market-oriented economic reforms continued and were accelerated after 1992 under strong state direction led by Deng Xiaoping.[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-\:1-38]

Considered a [watershed](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/watershed) event, reactions to the protests set limits on political expression in China that have lasted up to the present day.[^cite_note-40] The events remain one of the most sensitive and most widely censored topics in China.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2009-41][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoodman1994112-42]

The Chinese government has used numerous names for the event since 1989.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011634-43] As the events unfolded, it was labelled a "[counter revolutionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-revolutionary) rebellion", which was later changed to simply "riot", followed by "political turmoil" and "1989 storm".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011634-43] Currently, the CCP and the PRC term the events the "political turmoil between the spring and summer of 1989" (1989年春夏之交的政治风波; *1989 Nián Chūnxià Zhījiāo de Zhèngzhì Fēngbō*).[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45]

In China, the crackdown is known as the "June Fourth Incident" (六四事件; *liùsì shìjiàn*).[^cite_note-Quartz_Sonnad_20190603-46][^cite_note-China_Erase_History_–_LA_Times_JUNE_24,_2021-47] Outside mainland China, and among circles critical of the crackdown within mainland China, the crackdown is commonly referred to in Chinese as "June Fourth Massacre" (六四屠殺; *liùsì túshā*) and "June Fourth Crackdown" (六四鎮壓; *liùsì zhènyā*). To bypass censorship by the [Great Firewall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Firewall), alternative names have sprung up to describe the events on the Internet, such as May 35th, VIIV ([Roman numerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_numerals) for 6 and 4), Eight Squared (since 8<sup>2</sup>=64)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChina_tightens_information-48] and 8964 (in yymd format).[^cite_note-HKFP8964-49]

In English, the terms "Tiananmen Square Massacre", "Tiananmen Square Protests", and "Tiananmen Square Crackdown" are often used to describe the series of events. However, much of the violence in Beijing did not actually happen in Tiananmen, but outside the square along a stretch of [Chang'an Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'an_Avenue) only a few miles long, and especially near the [Muxidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muxidi) area.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaum1996283-50] The term also gives a misleading impression that demonstrations only happened in Beijing, when in fact, they occurred in many cities throughout China.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiles2009-18]

The [Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution) ended with chairman [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong)'s death in 1976 and the arrest of the [Gang of Four](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gang_of_Four).[^cite_note-Shen-2021-51][^cite_note-Peoples_Daily-1978-52] The movement, spearheaded by Mao, had caused severe damage to the country's initially diverse economic and social fabric.[^cite_note-53] As a result, the country was now mired in poverty as economic production slowed or came to a halt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaughton2007-54]

[Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping) proposed the idea of *[Boluan Fanzheng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boluan_Fanzheng)* ("bringing order out of chaos") to correct the mistakes of the Cultural Revolution.[^cite_note-Peoples_Daily-1978-52] He launched a comprehensive program to reform the Chinese economy called the [reform and opening up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up). Within several years, the country's focus on ideological purity was replaced by a concerted attempt to achieve material prosperity. To oversee his reform agenda, Deng promoted his allies to top government and party posts. [Zhao Ziyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhao_Ziyang) was named [Premier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China), the head of government, in September 1980, and [Hu Yaobang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Yaobang) became [CCP General Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) in 1982. Deng had believed that the "[iron rice bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_rice_bowl)" was too costly to maintain on the path to modernization and his reforms dismantled Mao-era guarantees such as lifetime employment in state-owned enterprises and associated workplace benefits, contributing to worker unrest and protests over layoffs and inflation.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56]

### Challenges to Reforms and Opening up

Deng's reforms aimed to decrease the state's role in the economy and gradually allow private production in agriculture and industry. By 1981, roughly 73% of rural farms had been de-collectivized, and 80% of state-owned enterprises were permitted to retain their profits.

While the reforms were generally well received by the public, concerns grew over a series of social problems which the changes brought about, including [corruption](19519245.md) and [nepotism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepotism) on the part of elite party bureaucrats.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaughton200791-57] The state-mandated pricing system, in place since the 1950s, had long kept prices fixed at low levels. The initial reforms created a [dual-track price system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual-track_price_system) where some prices were fixed while others were allowed to fluctuate. In a market with chronic shortages, price fluctuation allowed people with powerful connections to buy goods at low prices and sell at market prices. Party bureaucrats in charge of economic management had enormous incentives to engage in such [arbitrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbitrage).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001120-58] Discontent over corruption reached a fever pitch with the public; and many, particularly intellectuals, began to believe that only democratic reform and the rule of law could cure the country's ills.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001Chapter_5:_On_the_Eve_of_the_1989_Movement-59]

Following the 1988 meeting at their summer retreat of [Beidaihe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beidaihe), the party leadership under Deng [agreed to implement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_price_reform_in_China) a transition to a market-based pricing system from the previous dual-track price system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001127-60] News of the relaxation of price controls triggered waves of cash withdrawals, [panic buying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic_buying), and hoarding all over China.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001127-60][^cite_note-61] The government panicked and rescinded the price reforms in less than two weeks, but there was a pronounced impact for much longer. Inflation soared; official indices reported that the [Consumer Price Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_Price_Index) increased by 30% in Beijing between 1987 and 1988, leading to panic among salaried workers that they could no longer afford staple goods.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011600–601-62] Moreover, in the new market economy, unprofitable [state-owned enterprises](204768.md) were pressured to cut costs. This threatened a vast proportion of the population that relied on the "[iron rice bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_rice_bowl)", and instilled fear of potential layoffs coined in Chinese as "[xiagang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiagang)" (下岗), which would imply the loss of social benefits such as job security, medical care, and subsidised housing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011600–601-62]

### Social disenfranchisement and legitimacy crisis

See also: [New Enlightenment (China)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Enlightenment_\(China\))



A slogan inside the [Former Residence of Hu Yaobang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Former_Residence_of_Hu_Yaobang), a leading reformist whose death triggered the Tiananmen Square protests in 1989

In 1978, reformist leaders had envisioned that intellectuals would play a leading role in guiding the country through reforms, but this did not happen as planned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao200181-63] On one hand, the massive [New Enlightenment movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Enlightenment_\(China\)) led by intellectuals promoted a variety of [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_China) philosophies and values that challenged the socialist ideology, ranging from democracy, [humanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanism), [universal values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_value) such as freedom and human rights, to [Total Westernisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_Westernization);[^cite_note-Pei-2019-35][^cite_note-Li-2012-64] in response, since the beginning of the reforms, Deng Xiaoping in 1979 proposed the [Four Cardinal Principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Cardinal_Principles) to limit the [political liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_liberalisation).[^cite_note-65] On the other hand, despite the opening of new universities and increased enrollment,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao200182-66] the state-directed education system did not produce enough graduates to meet increased demand in the areas of agriculture, light industry, services, and foreign investment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao200184-67] The job market was especially limited for students specializing in social sciences and the humanities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao200182-66] Moreover, private companies no longer needed to accept students assigned to them by the state, and many high-paying jobs were offered based on nepotism and favouritism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao200189-68] Gaining a good state-assigned placement meant navigating a highly inefficient bureaucracy that gave power to officials who had little expertise in areas under their jurisdiction.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011600–601-62] Facing a dismal job market and limited chances of going abroad, intellectuals and students had a greater vested interest in political issues. Small study groups, such as the "Democracy Salon" ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters): 民主沙龙; [pinyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinyin): *Mínzhǔ Shālóng*) and the "Lawn Salon" (草地沙龙; *Cǎodì Shālóng*), began appearing on Beijing university campuses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001137-69] These organisations motivated the students to get involved politically.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001127-60]

Simultaneously, the party's nominally socialist ideology faced a legitimacy crisis as it gradually adopted capitalist practices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang200657-70] Private enterprise gave rise to profiteers who took advantage of lax regulations and who often flaunted their wealth in front of those who were less well off.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011600–601-62] Popular discontent was brewing over unfair wealth distribution. Greed, not skill, appeared to be the most crucial factor in success. There was widespread public disillusionment concerning the country's future. People wanted change, yet the power to define "the correct path" continued to rest solely in the unelected government's hands.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang200657-70]

The comprehensive and wide-ranging reforms created political differences over the pace of [marketisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marketization) and the control over the ideology that came with it, opening a deep chasm within the central leadership. The reformers ("the right", led by Hu Yaobang) favoured political liberalisation and a plurality of ideas as a channel to voice popular discontent and pressed for further reforms. The conservatives ("the left", led by [Chen Yun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Yun)) said that the reforms had gone too far and advocated a return to greater state control to ensure social stability and to better align with the party's socialist ideology. Both sides needed the backing of paramount leader Deng Xiaoping to carry out important policy decisions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71]

### 1986 student demonstrations

Main article: [1986 Chinese student demonstrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Chinese_student_demonstrations)

In mid-1986, astrophysics professor [Fang Lizhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fang_Lizhi) returned from a position at [Princeton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University) and began a personal tour of universities in China, speaking about liberty, human rights, and the [separation of powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers). Fang was part of a wide undercurrent within the elite intellectual community that thought China's poverty and underdevelopment, and the disaster of the Cultural Revolution, were a direct result of China's authoritarian political system and rigid command economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao200164,_215-72] The view that political reform was the only answer to China's ongoing problems gained widespread appeal among students, as Fang's recorded speeches became widely circulated throughout the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEE._Cheng200933-73] In response, Deng Xiaoping warned that Fang was blindly worshipping Western lifestyles, capitalism, and multi-party systems while undermining China's socialist ideology, traditional values, and the party's leadership.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEE._Cheng200933-73]

In December 1986, inspired by Fang and other "people-power" movements worldwide, student demonstrators staged protests against the slow pace of reform. The issues were wide-ranging and included demands for [economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization), democracy, and the rule of law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang200656–57-74] While the protests were initially contained in [Hefei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hefei), where Fang lived, they quickly spread to Shanghai, Beijing, and other major cities. Throughout the demonstrations, broadcasts by the [Voice of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America) news outlet became a key source of information for student protesters.[^cite_note-wapo-75]

General Secretary Hu Yaobang was blamed for showing a "soft" attitude and mishandling the protests, thus undermining social stability. He was denounced thoroughly by conservatives and was forced to resign as general secretary on 16 January 1987. The party began the "Anti-[bourgeois liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourgeois_liberalization) campaign", aiming at Hu, political liberalisation, and Western-inspired ideas in general.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpence1999685-76] The campaign stopped student protests and restricted political activity, but Hu remained popular among intellectuals, students, and Communist Party progressives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001138-77]

### Political reforms

Main article: [History of the People's Republic of China § Political reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China#Political_reforms)



[Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping) (1904–1997) was the [Paramount Leader of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramount_Leader_of_China).&#x20;

On 18 August 1980, [Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping) gave a speech titled "On the Reform of the Party and State Leadership System" ("党和国家领导制度改革") at a full meeting of the [CCP Politburo](51851.md) in Beijing, launching political reforms in China.[^cite_note-Deng-2018-78][^cite_note-Ng-Quinn-1982-79][^cite_note-Whyte-1993-80] He called for a systematic revision of China's constitution, criticizing bureaucracy, centralistion of power, and patriarchy, while proposing term limits for the leading positions in China and advocating [democratic centralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_centralism) and [collective leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_leadership).[^cite_note-Deng-2018-78][^cite_note-Ng-Quinn-1982-79][^cite_note-Whyte-1993-80] In December 1982, the fourth and current Constitution of China, known as the [1982 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_China), was passed by the [5th National People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5th_National_People's_Congress).[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82]

In the first half of 1986, Deng repeatedly called for the revival of political reforms, as further economic reforms were hindered by the original political system with an increasing trend of [corruption](19519245.md) and [economic inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality).[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] A five-man committee to study the feasibility of political reform was established in September 1986; the members included [Zhao Ziyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhao_Ziyang), [Hu Qili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Qili), [Tian Jiyun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tian_Jiyun), [Bo Yibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bo_Yibo) and [Peng Chong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peng_Chong).[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86] Deng's intention was to boost administrative efficiency, further separate responsibilities of the Party and the government, and eliminate bureaucracy.[^cite_note-Wu-2014a-87][^cite_note-Wu-2014b-88] Although he spoke in terms of the [rule of law](25166191.md) and [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy), Deng delimited the reforms within the [one-party system](102459.md) and opposed the implementation of Western-style [constitutionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutionalism).[^cite_note-Wu-2014b-88][^cite_note-Lim-2019-89]

In October 1987, at the [13th National Congress of the CCP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/13th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), Zhao Ziyang gave a report drafted by [Bao Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bao_Tong) on the political reforms.[^cite_note-Schram-1988-90][^cite_note-Dirlik-2019-91] In his speech titled "Advance Along the Road of [Socialism with Chinese characteristics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_with_Chinese_characteristics)" ("沿着有中国特色的社会主义道路前进"), Zhao argued that socialism in China was still in its [primary stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_stage_of_socialism) and, taking Deng's speech in 1980 as a guideline, detailed steps to be taken for political reform, including promoting the rule of law and the [separation of powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers), imposing de-centralisation, and improving the election system.[^cite_note-Wu-2014a-87][^cite_note-Schram-1988-90][^cite_note-Dirlik-2019-91] At this Congress, Zhao was elected to be the CCP General Secretary.[^cite_note-92]

## Funding and support

Main article: [Funding of student organizations during the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funding_of_student_organizations_during_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre)

During the demonstrations, protesters received a significant amount of support from domestic and outside sources.[^cite_note-Qiping90-93] The Chinese University in Hong Kong donated HK\$10,000 by early May,[^cite_note-Goldman94-94]<sup>: 313 </sup> and groups such as the [Hong Kong Alliance in Support of Patriotic Democratic Movements of China](1974713.md) were founded in support of the protests. Funding also came from the United States, Canada, Japan, Taiwan, Australia, and countries across Europe.[^cite_note-Zhang2001a-95] The [Chinese Alliance for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Alliance_for_Democracy) (CAD), a New York–based overseas Chinese [NGO](46539.md), provided financial support for the protests and organized rallies in both New York and Washington, D.C.[^cite_note-chenjie-96] In April, [Liu Xiaobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Xiaobo) and Hu Ping, one of CAD's leaders, jointly published an "Open Letter to Chinese University Students", urging protesters to unite, build on their achievements, and strive to advance freedom within their campuses.[^cite_note-Perry1-97]

## Beginning of the 1989 protests

### Death of Hu Yaobang

| Name | Origin and affiliation |
| --- | --- |
| [Chai Ling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chai_Ling) | [Shandong](105032.md); [Beijing Normal University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Normal_University) |
| [Wu'erkaixi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wu'erkaixi) (Örkesh) | [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang); [Beijing Normal University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Normal_University) |
| [Wang Dan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Dan_\(dissident\)) | Beijing; [Peking University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peking_University) |
| [Feng Congde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feng_Congde) | [Sichuan](65185.md); [Peking University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peking_University) |
| [Shen Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shen_Tong) | Beijing; [Peking University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peking_University) |
| [Wang Youcai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Youcai) | [Zhejiang](96602.md); [Peking University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peking_University) |
| [Li Lu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Lu) | [Hebei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebei); [Nanjing University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanjing_University) |
| [Zhou Yongjun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Yongjun) | [China University of Political Science and Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_University_of_Political_Science_and_Law) |

After Hu Yaobang's sudden death of a heart attack on 15 April 1989, students reacted strongly, most believing his death was related to his forced resignation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun1989-98] Hu's death provided the initial impetus for students to gather in large numbers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPan2008274-99] On university campuses, many posters appeared eulogizing Hu, calling for honouring Hu's legacy. Within days, most posters were about broader political issues, such as corruption, democracy, and freedom of the press.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001147-100] Small, spontaneous gatherings to mourn Hu began on 15 April around the [Monument to the People's Heroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_to_the_People's_Heroes) at [Tiananmen Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiananmen_Square). On the same day, many students at [Peking University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peking_University) (PKU) and [Tsinghua University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsinghua_University) joined the gathering in Tiananmen Square in a piecemeal fashion. Small, organised student gatherings also took place in [Xi'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi'an) and Shanghai on 16 April. On 17 April, students at the [China University of Political Science and Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_University_of_Political_Science_and_Law) (CUPL) made a large [wreath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wreath) to commemorate Hu Yaobang. Its wreath-laying ceremony was on 17 April, and a larger-than-expected crowd assembled.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001148-101] At 5 pm, 500 CUPL students reached the eastern gate of the [Great Hall of the People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Hall_of_the_People), near Tiananmen Square, to mourn Hu. The gathering featured speakers from various backgrounds who gave public orations commemorating Hu and discussed social problems. However, it was soon deemed obstructive to the Great Hall's operation, so police tried to persuade the students to disperse.

Starting on the night of 17 April, three thousand PKU students marched from the campus towards Tiananmen Square, and soon nearly a thousand students from Tsinghua joined. Upon arrival, they soon joined forces with those already gathered at the square. As its size grew, the gathering gradually evolved into a protest, as students began to draft a list of pleas and suggestions (the Seven Demands) for the government:

1. Affirm Hu Yaobang's views on democracy and freedom as correct.

2. Admit that the campaigns against [spiritual pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Spiritual_Pollution_Campaign) and bourgeois liberalisation had been wrong.

3. Publish information on the income of state leaders and their family members.

4. Allow privately run newspapers and stop press censorship.

5. Increase funding for education and raise intellectuals' pay.

6. End restrictions on demonstrations in Beijing.

7. Provide objective coverage of students in official media.

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001-102]

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001148-101]

On the morning of 18 April, students remained in the square. Some gathered around the Monument to the People's Heroes, singing patriotic songs and listening to student organizers' impromptu speeches.

Others gathered at the Great Hall. Meanwhile, a few thousand students gathered at [Xinhua Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinhua_Gate), the entrance to [Zhongnanhai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhongnanhai), the seat of the party leadership, where they demanded dialogue with the administration. On 19 April, they attempted to storm Zhongnanhai several times but were pushed back by police.[^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104] Meanwhile, other students held aloft a banner that read "Freedom & Democracy Enlightenment" on the Monument, under a giant portrait of Hu Yaobang.[^cite_note-105]

On 20 April, most students had been persuaded to leave Xinhua Gate. To disperse about 200 students that remained, police used batons; minor clashes were reported. Many students felt abused by the police, and rumours about police brutality spread quickly. The incident angered students on campus, where those who were not politically active decided to join the protests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001149-106] Additionally, a group of workers calling themselves the [Beijing Workers' Autonomous Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Workers'_Autonomous_Federation) issued two handbills challenging the central leadership.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalderGong19931–2-107]

Hu's [state funeral](714370.md) took place on 22 April. On the evening of 21 April, some 100,000 students marched on Tiananmen Square, ignoring orders from Beijing municipal authorities that the square was to be closed for the funeral. The funeral, which took place inside the Great Hall and was attended by the leadership, was broadcast live to the students. General Secretary [Zhao Ziyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhao_Ziyang) delivered the eulogy. The funeral seemed rushed, lasting only 40 minutes, as emotions ran high in the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71][^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001152–153-108][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi201021_May_entry-109]

Security cordoned off the east entrance to the Great Hall of the People, but several students pressed forward. A few were allowed to cross the police line. Three of these students, [Zhou Yongjun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Yongjun), [Guo Haifeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guo_Haifeng?action=edit\&redlink=1), and Zhang Zhiyong, knelt on the steps of the Great Hall to present a petition and demanded to see Premier [Li Peng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Peng).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001153-110][^cite_note-113] Standing beside them, a fourth student ([Wu'erkaixi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wu'erkaixi)) made a brief, emotional speech begging for Li Peng to come out and speak with them. The larger number of students still in the square but outside the cordon were at times emotional, shouting demands or slogans and rushing toward police. Wu'erkaixi calmed the crowd as they waited for the Premier to emerge. However, no leaders emerged from the Great Hall, leaving the students disappointed and angry; some called for a classroom boycott.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001153-110]

On 21 April, students began organizing under the banners of formal organisations. On 23 April, in a meeting of about 40 students from 21 universities, the [Beijing Students' Autonomous Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Students'_Autonomous_Federation) (also known as the Union) was formed. It elected CUPL student [Zhou Yongjun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Yongjun) as chair. [Wang Dan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Dan_\(dissident\)) and [Wu'erkaixi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wu'erkaixi) also emerged as leaders. The Union then called for a general classroom boycott at all Beijing universities. Such an independent organisation operating outside of party jurisdiction alarmed the leadership.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001154-114]

### Rioting on 22 April

On 22 April, near dusk, serious rioting broke out in [Changsha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changsha) and [Xi'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi'an).

In Xi'an, arson by rioters destroyed cars and houses, and looting occurred in shops near the city's Xihua Gate. At Xincheng Square, a broadcast of Hu Yaobang's memorial was disrupted by rioters who attempted to force their way into a provincial government compound, smashing windows and setting parts of it on fire.[^cite_note-gaz-115] They also stoned a bus filled with tourists near the square.[^cite_note-gaz-115] The rioting destroyed 20 houses and 10 vehicles, and 130 policemen were reported injured.[^cite_note-gaz-115]

In Changsha, crowds following a student march smashed windows of shops and cars, and a bus was reportedly commandeered.[^cite_note-116] Chinese television showed images of the damage and injuries to shopkeepers.[^cite_note-117] 38 stores were ransacked in the rioting. Over 350 people were arrested in both Changsha and Xi'an for looting.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZ._Zhao2009-118]

In Wuhan, university students organised protests against the provincial government. As the situation became more volatile nationally, Zhao Ziyang called numerous meetings of the [Politburo Standing Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politburo_Standing_Committee) (PSC). Zhao stressed three points: discourage students from further protests and ask them to go back to class, use all measures necessary to combat rioting, and open forms of dialogue with students at different levels of government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZ._Zhao2009-118] Premier Li Peng called upon Zhao to condemn protestors and recognize the need to take more serious action. Zhao dismissed Li's views. Despite calls for him to remain in Beijing, Zhao left for a scheduled state visit to [North Korea](21255.md) on 23 April.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELiu1990505–521-119]

### 26 April Editorial

Main article: [26 April Editorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_26_Editorial)

Zhao Ziyang

Li Peng

General Secretary [Zhao Ziyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhao_Ziyang) (left) who pushed for dialogue with students and Premier [Li Peng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Peng) (right) who declared martial law and backed military action

Zhao's departure to North Korea left Li Peng as the acting executive authority in Beijing. On 24 April, Li Peng and the PSC met with Beijing Party Secretary [Li Ximing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Ximing) and mayor [Chen Xitong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Xitong) to gauge the situation at the square. The municipal officials wanted a quick resolution to the crisis and framed the protests as a conspiracy to overthrow China's political system and prominent party leaders, including Deng Xiaoping. In Zhao's absence, the PSC agreed to take firm action against the protesters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELiu1990505–521-119] On the morning of 25 April, President [Yang Shangkun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang_Shangkun) and Premier [Li Peng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Peng) met with Deng at the latter's residence. Deng endorsed a hardline stance and said an appropriate warning must be disseminated via mass media to curb further demonstrations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011603–606-120] The meeting firmly established the first official evaluation of the protests, and highlighted Deng's having "final say" on important issues. Li Peng subsequently ordered Deng's views to be drafted as a communique and issued to all high-level Communist Party officials to mobilize the party apparatus against protesters.

On 26 April, the party's official newspaper *[People's Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Daily)* issued a front-page editorial titled "[It is necessary to take a clear-cut stand against disturbances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_26_Editorial)". The language in the editorial effectively branded the student movement to be an anti-party, anti-government revolt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE26_April_Editorial1995-121] The editorial invoked memories of the Cultural Revolution, using similar rhetoric that had been used during the [1976 Tiananmen Incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Tiananmen_Incident)—an event that was initially branded an anti-government conspiracy but was later rehabilitated as "patriotic" under Deng's leadership.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71] The article enraged students, who interpreted it as a direct indictment of the protests and its cause. The editorial backfired: instead of scaring students into submission, it antagonised the students and put them squarely against the government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001155-122] The editorial's polarizing nature made it a major sticking point for the remainder of the protests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011603–606-120]

### 27 April demonstrations

Main article: [27 April demonstrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_27_demonstrations)



[Han Dongfang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Dongfang), founder of the [Beijing Workers' Autonomous Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Workers'_Autonomous_Federation)

Organized by the Union on 27 April, some 50,000–100,000 students from all Beijing universities marched through the streets of the capital to Tiananmen Square, breaking through lines set up by police, and receiving widespread public support along the way, particularly from factory workers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71] The student leaders, eager to show the patriotic nature of the movement, also toned down anti-Communist slogans, choosing to present a message of "anti-corruption" and "anti-cronyism", but "pro-party".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001155-122] In a twist of irony, student factions who genuinely called for the overthrow of the Communist Party gained traction due to the 26 April editorial.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001155-122]

The stunning success of the march forced the government into making concessions and meeting with student representatives. On 29 April, [State Council](44498.md) spokesman [Yuan Mu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_Mu) met with appointed representatives of government-sanctioned student associations. While the talks discussed a wide range of issues, including the editorial, the Xinhua Gate incident, and freedom of the press, they achieved few substantive results. Independent student leaders such as Wu'erkaixi refused to attend.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001157-123]

The government's tone grew increasingly conciliatory when Zhao Ziyang returned from [Pyongyang](47948.md) on 30 April and reasserted his authority. In Zhao's view, the hardliner approach was not working, and the concession was the only alternative.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001156-124] Zhao asked that the press be allowed to positively report the movement and delivered two sympathetic speeches on 3–4 May. In the speeches, Zhao said that the students' concerns about corruption were legitimate and that the student movement was patriotic in nature.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011608-125] The speeches essentially negated the message presented by 26 April Editorial. While some 100,000 students marched on the streets of Beijing on 4 May to commemorate the [May Fourth Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_Fourth_Movement) and repeated demands from earlier marches, many students were satisfied with the government's concessions. On 4 May, all Beijing universities except PKU and BNU announced the end of the classroom boycott. Subsequently, most students began to lose interest in the movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001159-126]

## Escalation of the protests

### Preparing for dialogue

Main article: [Dialogue between students and the government during the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialogue_between_students_and_the_government_during_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre)

The government was divided on how to respond to the movement as early as mid-April. After Zhao Ziyang's return from North Korea, tensions between the progressive camp and the conservative camp intensified. Those who supported continued dialogue and a soft approach with students rallied behind Zhao Ziyang, while hardliner conservatives opposed the movement rallied behind Premier Li Peng. Zhao and Li clashed at a PSC meeting on 1 May. Li maintained that the need for stability overrode all else, while Zhao said that the party should show support for increased democracy and transparency. Zhao pushed the case for further dialogue.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVogel2011608-125]

In preparation for dialogue, the Union elected representatives to a formal delegation. However, there was some friction as the Union leaders were reluctant to let the delegation unilaterally take control of the movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001161-127] The movement was slowed by a change to a more deliberate approach, fractured by internal discord, and increasingly diluted by declining engagement from the student body at large. In this context, a group of charismatic leaders, including Wang Dan and Wu'erkaixi, desired to regain momentum. They also distrusted the government's offers of dialogue, dismissing them as merely a ploy designed to play for time and pacify the students. To break from the moderate and incremental approach now adopted by other major student leaders, these few began calling for a return to more confrontational tactics. They settled on a plan of mobilizing students for a hunger strike that would begin on 13 May.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001161–162-128] Early attempts to mobilize others to join them met with only modest success until [Chai Ling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chai_Ling) made an emotional appeal on the night before the strike was scheduled to begin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001163-129]

### Hunger strikes begin

Main article: [Tiananmen hunger strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiananmen_hunger_strikes)



Photo of [Pu Zhiqiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pu_Zhiqiang), student protester at Tiananmen, taken on 10 May. The words say: "We want the freedom of newspapers, freedom of associations, also to support the *[World Economic Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Economic_Herald)*, and support those just journalists."

Students began the hunger strike on 13 May, two days before the highly publicised [state visit](327283.md) by Soviet leader [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev). Knowing that Gorbachev's welcoming ceremony was scheduled to be held on the square, student leaders wanted to use the hunger strike to force the government into meeting their demands. Moreover, the hunger strike gained widespread sympathy from the population at large and earned the student movement the moral high ground that it sought.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEE._Cheng2009612–614-130] By the afternoon of 13 May, some 300,000 were gathered at the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001167-131]

Inspired by the events in Beijing, protests and strikes began at universities in other cities, with many students travelling to Beijing to join the demonstration. Generally, the Tiananmen Square demonstration was well ordered, with daily marches of students from various Beijing-area colleges displaying their support of the classroom boycott and the protesters' demands. The students sang *[The Internationale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Internationale)*, the world socialist anthem, on their way to, and while at, the square.[^cite_note-132]

Afraid that the movement would spin out of control, Deng Xiaoping ordered the square to be cleared for Gorbachev's visit. Executing Deng's request, Zhao again used a soft approach and directed his subordinates to coordinate negotiations with students immediately.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEE._Cheng2009612–614-130] Zhao believed he could appeal to the students' patriotism. The students understood that signs of internal turmoil during the Sino-Soviet summit would embarrass the nation and not just the government. On the morning of 13 May, [Yan Mingfu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yan_Mingfu), head of the Communist Party's [United Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_\(China\)), called an emergency meeting, gathering prominent student leaders and intellectuals, including [Liu Xiaobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Xiaobo), [Chen Ziming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Ziming), and [Wang Juntao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Juntao).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001164-133] Yan said that the government was prepared to hold an immediate dialogue with student representatives. The Tiananmen welcoming ceremony for Gorbachev would be cancelled whether or not the students withdrew—in effect removing the bargaining power the students thought they possessed. The announcement sent the student leadership into disarray.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001165-134]

### Mikhail Gorbachev's visit

Main article: [1989 Sino-Soviet Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Sino-Soviet_Summit)

Press restrictions were loosened significantly from early to mid-May. State media began broadcasting footage sympathetic to protesters and the movement, including the hunger strikers. On 14 May, intellectuals led by [Dai Qing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dai_Qing) gained permission from [Hu Qili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Qili) to bypass government censorship and air the progressive views of the nation's intellectuals in the *[Guangming Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangming_Daily_\(China\))*. The intellectuals then issued an urgent appeal for the students to leave the square in an attempt to deescalate the conflict.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001167-131] However, many students believed that the intellectuals were speaking for the government and refused to move. That evening, formal negotiations took place between government representatives led by Yan Mingfu and student representatives led by [Shen Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shen_Tong) and Xiang Xiaoji. Yan affirmed the student movement's patriotic nature and pleaded for the students to withdraw from the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001165-134] While Yan's apparent sincerity for compromise satisfied some students, the meeting grew increasingly chaotic as competing student factions relayed uncoordinated and incoherent demands to the leadership. Shortly after student leaders learned that the event had not been broadcast nationally, as initially promised by the government, the meeting fell apart.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001169-135] Yan then personally went to the square to appeal to the students, even offering himself to be held hostage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71] Yan also took the student's pleas to Li Peng the next day, asking Li to consider formally retracting the 26 April Editorial and rebranding the movement as "patriotic and democratic"; Li refused.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi201015_May_entry-136]

The students remained in the square during the Gorbachev visit; his welcoming ceremony was held at the airport. The Sino-Soviet summit, the first of its kind in [some 30 years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split), marked the normalisation of Sino-Soviet relations and was seen as a breakthrough of tremendous historical significance for China's leaders. However, its smooth proceedings were derailed by the student movement; this created a major embarrassment ("loss of [face](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Face_\(sociological_concept\))")[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoberts2011300-137] for the leadership on the global stage and drove many moderates in government onto a more hardline path.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001170-138] The summit between Deng and Gorbachev took place at the [Great Hall of the People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Hall_of_the_People) amid the backdrop of commotion and protest in the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEE._Cheng2009612–614-130] When Gorbachev met with Zhao on 16 May, Zhao told him, and by extension the international press, that Deng was still the "paramount authority" in China. Deng felt that this remark was Zhao's attempt to shift blame for mishandling the movement to him. Zhao's defence against this accusation was that privately informing world leaders that Deng was the true centre of power was standard operating procedure; Li Peng had made nearly identical private statements to US president [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) in February 1989.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarotte2012165-139] Nevertheless, the statement marked a decisive split between the country's two most senior leaders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEE._Cheng2009612–614-130]

### Gathering momentum

From 17 May to 18 May, about a million Beijing residents, including PLA personnel, police officers, and lower party officials, demonstrated to call for the government to resume negotiations with the students.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001171-14] Many grassroots Party and [Youth League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Youth_League) organisations, as well as government-sponsored labour unions, encouraged their membership to demonstrate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001171-14] In addition, several of China's non-Communist parties sent a letter to Li Peng to support the students. The [Chinese Red Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Cross_Society_of_China) issued a special notice and sent in many personnel to provide medical services to the hunger strikers on the square. After the departure of [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev), many foreign journalists remained in the Chinese capital to cover the protests, shining an international spotlight on the movement. Western governments urged Beijing to exercise restraint.

The movement, on the wane at the end of April, now regained momentum. By 17 May, as students from across the country poured into the capital to join the movement, protests of various sizes occurred in some 400 Chinese cities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas2006-16] Students demonstrated at provincial party headquarters in Fujian, Hubei, and Xinjiang. Without a clearly articulated official position from the Beijing leadership, local authorities did not know how to respond. Because the demonstrations now included a wide array of social groups, each having its own set of grievances, it became increasingly unclear with whom the government should negotiate and what the demands were. The government, still split on how to deal with the movement, saw its authority and legitimacy gradually erode as the hunger strikers took the limelight and gained widespread sympathy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001171-14] These combined circumstances put immense pressure on the authorities to act, and martial law was discussed as an appropriate response.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001181-140] Student leaders were put under close surveillance by the authorities; traffic cameras were used to perform surveillance on the square; and nearby restaurants, and wherever students gathered, were wiretapped.[^cite_note-4-corners-tremble-and-obey-141] This surveillance led to the identification, capture, and punishment of protest participants.[^cite_note-surveillance-politics-142]

The situation seemed intractable, and the weight of taking decisive action fell on paramount leader Deng Xiaoping. Matters came to a head on 17 May during a [Politburo Standing Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politburo_Standing_Committee) meeting at Deng's residence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi201017_May_entry-143] At the meeting, Zhao Ziyang's concessions-based strategy, which called for the retraction of the 26 April Editorial, was thoroughly criticised.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan20022–48-144] Li Peng, [Yao Yilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yao_Yilin), and Deng asserted that by making a conciliatory speech to the [Asian Development Bank](2512.md), on 4 May, Zhao had exposed divisions within the top leadership and emboldened the students.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan20022–48-144][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZ._Zhao200925–34-145][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacFarquhar2011443-146] Deng warned that "there is no way to back down now without the situation spiraling out of control", and so "the decision is to move troops into Beijing to declare [martial law](50477.md)"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZ._Zhao200928-147] as a show of the government's no-tolerance stance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan20022–48-144] To justify martial law, the demonstrators were described as tools of "[bourgeois liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourgeois_liberalism)" advocates who were pulling strings behind the scenes, as well as tools of elements within the party who wished to further their personal ambitions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiles1997-148] For the rest of his life, Zhao Ziyang maintained that the decision was ultimately in Deng's hands: among the five PSC members present at the meeting, he and [Hu Qili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Qili) opposed the imposition of martial law, Li Peng and Yao Yilin firmly supported it, and [Qiao Shi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiao_Shi) remained carefully neutral and noncommittal. Deng appointed the latter three to carry out the decision.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZ._Zhao200928–30-149]

On the evening of 17 May, the PSC met at Zhongnanhai to finalize plans for martial law. At the meeting, Zhao announced that he was ready to "take leave", citing he could not bring himself to carry out martial law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan20022–48-144] The elders in attendance at the meeting, [Bo Yibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bo_Yibo) and Yang Shangkun, urged the PSC to follow Deng's orders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan20022–48-144] Zhao did not consider the inconclusive PSC vote to have legally binding implications for martial law;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIgnatius2009x-150] Yang Shangkun, in his capacity as [Vice Chairman of the Central Military Commission](46959142.md), mobilised the military to move into the capital.[^cite_note-151]

Li Peng [met with students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_and_Government_Dialogue_during_the_1989_Student_Movement#May_18:_meeting_with_Li_Peng) for the first time on 18 May in an attempt to placate public concern over the hunger strike.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001181-140] During the talks, student leaders again demanded that the government rescind the 26 April Editorial and affirm the student movement as "patriotic". Li Peng said the government's main concern was sending the hunger strikers to hospitals. The discussions were confrontational and yielded little substantive progress,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook199841-152] but gained student leaders prominent airtime on national television.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPye1990343-153] By this point, those calling for the overthrow of the party and Li Peng and Deng became prominent both in Beijing and in other cities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook199842–43-154]



[Wen Jiabao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wen_Jiabao), then chief of the [Party's General Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Office_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), accompanied Zhao Ziyang to meet students in the square, surviving the political purge of the party's liberals and later serving as [Premier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) from 2003 to 2013.

In the early morning of 19 May, Zhao Ziyang went to Tiananmen in what became his political [swan song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swan_song). He was accompanied by [Wen Jiabao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wen_Jiabao). Li Peng also went to the square but left shortly thereafter. At 4\:50 am Zhao made a speech with a bullhorn to a crowd of students, urging them to end the hunger strike.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIgnatius2009xv-155] He told the students that they were still young and urged them to stay healthy and not to sacrifice themselves without due concern for their futures. Zhao's emotional speech was applauded by some students. It would be his last public appearance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIgnatius2009xv-155]

> Students, we came too late. We are sorry. You talk about us, criticize us, it is all necessary. The reason that I came here is not to ask you to forgive us. All I want to say is that students are getting very weak. It is the 7th day since you went on a hunger strike. You can't continue like this. \[...] You are still young, there are still many days yet to come, you must live healthily, and see the day when China accomplishes the [Four Modernisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Modernisations). You are not like us. We are already old. It doesn't matter to us anymore.

— [Zhao Ziyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhao_Ziyang) at Tiananmen Square, 19 May 1989

### Outside Beijing

Main article: [Gifts and donations from outside Mainland China during the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gifts_and_donations_from_outside_Mainland_China_during_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests)

On 19 April, the editors of the *[World Economic Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Economic_Herald)*, a magazine close to reformists, decided to publish a commemorative section on Hu. Inside was an article by [Yan Jiaqi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yan_Jiaqi), which commented favourably on the Beijing student protests, and called for a reassessment of Hu's 1987 purge. Sensing the conservative political trends in Beijing, [Jiang Zemin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Zemin) demanded that the article be censored, and many newspapers were printed with a blank page.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWright1990121–132-156] Jiang then suspended lead editor [Qin Benli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qin_Benli), his decisive action earning the trust of conservative party elders, who praised Jiang's loyalty.

On 27 May, over 300,000 people in Hong Kong gathered at [Happy Valley Racecourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Happy_Valley_Racecourse) for a gathering called the [Concert for Democracy in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concert_for_Democracy_in_China) ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 民主歌聲獻中華). Many Hong Kong celebrities sang songs and expressed their support for the students in Beijing.[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-concert-158] The following day, a procession of 1.5 million people, one fourth of Hong Kong's population, led by [Martin Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Lee), [Szeto Wah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Szeto_Wah), and other organisation leaders, paraded through [Hong Kong Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Island).[^cite_note-aothervastcrowd-159] Across the world, especially where ethnic Chinese lived, people gathered and protested. Many governments, including those of the United States and Japan, issued travel warnings against travelling to China.

## Military action

Main article: [People's Liberation Army at the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_at_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre)

### Martial law

The Chinese government declared [martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_from_the_State_Council_on_the_Imposition_of_Martial_Law_in_Parts_of_Beijing) on 20 May and [mobilised at least 30 divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_at_Tiananmen_Square_protests_of_1989#Mobilization) from five of the country's [seven military regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PLA_Military_Region).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWu200930–31-160] At least 14 of the PLA's 24 army corps contributed troops.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWu200930–31-160] Guangzhou's civil aviation authorities suspended civil airline travel to prepare for transporting military units.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook199880–82-161]

The army's initial entry into the capital was blocked in the suburbs by throngs of protesters. Seeing no way forward, the authorities ordered the army to withdraw on 24 May. All government forces then retreated to bases outside the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERichelsonEvans1999-25][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas2006-16]

Defying martial law, students entertained themselves with music, drinking, and smoking on the square.[^cite_note-162] At the same time, internal divisions intensified within the student movement itself. By late May, the students became increasingly disorganised with no clear leadership or unified course of action. Moreover, Tiananmen Square was overcrowded and facing serious hygiene problems. [Hou Dejian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hou_Dejian) suggested an open election of the student leadership to speak for the movement but was met with opposition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71] Meanwhile, [Wang Dan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Dan_\(dissident\)) moderated his position, ostensibly sensing the impending military action and its consequences. He advocated for a temporary withdrawal from Tiananmen Square to re-group on campus, but this was opposed by hardline student factions who wanted to hold the square. The increasing internal friction would lead to struggles for control of the loudspeakers in the middle of the square in a series of "mini-coups": whoever controlled the loudspeakers was "in charge" of the movement. Some students would wait at the train station to greet arrivals of students from other parts of the country in an attempt to enlist factional support.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71] Student groups began accusing each other of ulterior motives, such as collusion with the government and trying to gain personal fame from the movement. Some students even tried to oust Chai Ling and Feng Congde from their leadership positions in an attempted kidnapping, an action Chai called a "well-organised and premeditated plot".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71]

Unlike more moderate student leaders, Chai seemed willing to allow the student movement to end in a violent confrontation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanHua1990298-163] [In an interview given in late May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chai_Ling#The_Gate_of_Heavenly_Peace_documentary), Chai stated:

> What we actually are hoping for is bloodshed, the moment when the government is ready to brazenly butcher the people. Only when the Square is awash with blood will the people of China open their eyes.

— Chai Ling, *[The Gate of Heavenly Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gate_of_Heavenly_Peace_\(film\))*

However, she felt that she was unable to convince her fellow students of this.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanHua1990327-164] She also claimed that her expectation of a violent crackdown was something she had heard from [Li Lu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Lu) and not an idea of her own.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChai2011165-165]

On 30 May, the protesters erected the 10-metre-tall [Goddess of Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goddess_of_Democracy) at Tiananmen Square made of foam and papier-mâché.

### 1 June

#### Li Peng's Report

On 1 June, [Li Peng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Peng) issued a report titled "On the True Nature of the Turmoil", which was circulated to every member of the Politburo.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001434-166] The report concludes that the demonstrators' leadership, referred to as a "tiny minority", had "organised and plotted the turmoil", and that they were using the square as a base to provoke conflict in order to create an international impact.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001435-167] It also maintains that they had formed connections with criminal elements and used funding from foreign and domestic sources to improve their communications equipment and procure weapons.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001437-168]

#### MSS Report

On the same day, another report, issued by [Ministry of State Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_State_Security_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) chief [Jia Chunwang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jia_Chunwang), was submitted to the party leadership, and likewise sent to every member of the Politburo, as well as to senior [Party elders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eight_Elders), including [Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping), [Li Xiannian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Xiannian) and [Chen Yun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Yun).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001445-169]

The report emphasized the danger of infiltration of [bourgeois liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourgeois_liberalism) into China and the negative effect that Western ideological influence, particularly from the United States, had on the students.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001446-170] The MSS had determined that the United States had infiltrated the student movement by various means,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001447-171] including the use of the U.S. government-owned [Voice of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America) radio station as an instrument of [psychological warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_warfare), as well as the cultivation of pro-American ideologies among Chinese students studying abroad as a long-term strategy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001449-172] Furthermore, the report also resolved that U.S. intelligence had made efforts to get close to leaders of several Chinese institutions; according to the report, a CIA agent from the U.S. Embassy had nearly fifty contacts between 1981 and 1988, fifteen of whom were associated with the Economic Restructuring Commission.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001450-173] The report advocated for immediate military action,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001455-462-174] and was viewed as providing one of the best justifications for it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001445-169]

### 2–3 June

In conjunction with the plan to clear the square by force, the Politburo received word from army headquarters stating that troops were ready to help stabilize the capital and that they understood the necessity and legality of martial law to overcome the turmoil.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001349-175]

On 2 June, with increasing action on the part of protesters, the government saw that it was time to act. Protests broke out as newspapers published articles that called for the students to leave Tiananmen Square and end the movement. Many of the students in the square were not willing to leave and were outraged by the articles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001353-176] They were also outraged by the *Beijing Daily*'s 1 June article "Tiananmen, I Cry for You", which was written by a fellow student who had become disillusioned with the movement, as he thought it was chaotic and disorganised.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001353-176] In response to the articles, thousands of students lined the streets of Beijing to protest against leaving the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001362-177]

Three intellectuals—[Liu Xiaobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Xiaobo), Zhou Duo, and Gao Xin—and Taiwanese singer [Hou Dejian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hou_Dejian) declared a second hunger strike to revive the movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook199894-178] After weeks of occupying the square, the students were tired, and internal rifts opened between moderate and hardline student groups.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMathews1989-179] In their declaration speech, the hunger strikers openly criticised the government's suppression of the movement, to remind the students that their cause was worth fighting for and pushing them to continue their occupation of the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001363-180]

On 2 June, Deng Xiaoping and several [party elders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eight_Elders) met with the three PSC members—[Li Peng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Peng), [Qiao Shi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiao_Shi), and [Yao Yilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yao_Yilin)—who remained after Zhao Ziyang and Hu Qili had been ousted. The committee members agreed to clear the square so "the riot can be halted and order be restored to the Capital".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWu:_Clear_the_Square-181][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001355–362-182] They also agreed that the square needed to be cleared as peacefully as possible; but if protesters did not cooperate, the troops would be authorised to use force to complete the job.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001362-177] That day, state-run newspapers reported that troops were positioned in ten key areas in the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001362-177][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMathews1989-179] Units of the [27th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/27th_Army_\(People's_Republic_of_China\)), [65th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/65th_Army_\(People's_Republic_of_China\)), and 24th armies were [secretly moved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_at_Tiananmen_Square_protests_of_1989#Enforcement_of_martial_law) into the Great Hall of the People on the west side of the square and the [Ministry of Public Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Public_Security_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) compound east of the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWu:_Troop_Movements-183]

On the evening of 2 June, an accident occurred in which a [PAP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Armed_Police) jeep ran onto a sidewalk, killing three civilian pedestrians and injuring a fourth. This incident sparked fear that the army and the police were trying to advance into Tiananmen Square.[^cite_note-184] Student leaders issued emergency orders to set up roadblocks at major intersections to prevent the entry of troops into the centre of the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001482-185]

On the morning of 3 June, students and citizens intercepted and questioned a busload of plainclothed soldiers at Xinjiekou. Isolated pockets of soldiers were similarly surrounded and interrogated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001483-186][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71]

The soldiers were beaten by the crowd, as were Beijing security personnel who attempted to aid the soldiers. Some of the soldiers were kidnapped when they attempted to head for the hospital.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001482-185] Several other buses carrying weapons, gear, and supplies were intercepted and boarded around Tiananmen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001482-185]

At 1 pm, a crowd intercepted one of these buses at Liubukou, and several men raised military helmets on bayonets to show the rest of the crowd.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001484-187] At 2\:30 pm, a clash broke out between protesters and police.[^cite_note-Class_Struggle:_China's_Harsh_Actions_Threaten_to_Set_Back_10-Year_Reform_Drive-188][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001482-185] The police attempted to disperse the crowd with tear gas, but demonstrators counterattacked and threw rocks, forcing them to retreat inside the Zhongnanhai compound through the west gate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001484-187][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristof:_Residents_Block_Army-189][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71]

At 5\:30 pm, several thousand troops awaiting orders began to retreat from the Great Hall of the People.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001484-187][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERichelsonEvans1999-25] That evening, the government leaders continued to monitor the situation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETimothy_Brook_interview2006-190]

### &#x20;3–4 June

| External videos |
| --- |
| video icon [BBC News archive footage](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kMKvxJ-Js3A) |

In the evening on 3 June, the government issued an emergency announcement urging citizens to "stay off the streets and away from Tiananmen Square".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002489-191] Meanwhile, protesters made their own broadcasts across various university campuses in Beijing to call for students and citizens to arm themselves and assemble at intersections and the Square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002489-191]

#### Chang'an Avenue



The [Type 59](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_59_tank) [main battle tank](26595097.md), here on display at the [Military Museum of the Chinese People's Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Museum_of_the_Chinese_People's_Revolution) in western Beijing, was deployed by the [People's Liberation Army](66890.md) on 3 June 1989.



[Type 63 armoured personnel carrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_63_\(armoured_personnel_carrier\)) deployed by the [People's Liberation Army](66890.md) in Beijing in 1989



[Type 56 assault rifles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_56_assault_rifle), used by soldiers during the crackdown

On 3 June, at 8\:00 p.m., the [38th Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/38th_Army_\(People's_Republic_of_China\)), led by interim commander [Zhang Meiyuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Meiyuan), began to advance from military office compounds in Shijingshan and Fentai District in western Beijing along the western extension of Chang'an Avenue toward the square to the east.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002492-192] At 9\:30 p.m, this army encountered a blockade set up by protesters at [Gongzhufen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gongzhufen) in [Haidian District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haidian_District), and made an attempt to break through.[^cite_note-tp-193] Fighting erupted when protesters, in an effort to push the army back, threw rocks, molotov cocktails, and other objects at troops armed with anti-riot gear, who began firing rubber bullets and tear gas and attacking with electric prods and batons.[^cite_note-Nepstad-194][^cite_note-vs-195]

Other troops fired warning shots into the air, which was ineffective.[^cite_note-tp-193] At 10\:10 pm, an army officer picked up a megaphone and urged the protesters to disperse.[^cite_note-tp-193]

At about 10\:30 p.m., still being pummelled by rocks thrown by protesters, the 38th Army troops opened fire with live ammunition.[^cite_note-tp-193] The crowds were stunned that the army was using live ammunition and fell back towards [Muxidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muxidi) Bridge.[^cite_note-tp-193][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESuettinger200460-196] The troops used [expanding bullets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expanding_bullets),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas2006-16] prohibited by international law[^cite_note-197] for use in warfare between countries but not for other uses.[^cite_note-198]

The advance of the army was again halted by another blockade at [Muxidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muxidi), about 5 km west of the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohn_Pomfret_interview2006-199] After protesters repelled an attempt by an anti-riot brigade to storm the bridge,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002492-192] regular troops advanced on the crowd and turned their weapons on them. Soldiers alternated between shooting into the air and firing directly at protesters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002493-200][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETimothy_Brook_interview2006-190][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohn_Pomfret_interview2006-199] As the army advanced, fatalities were recorded along Chang'an Avenue.
