The largest number occurred in the two-mile stretch of road running from Muxidi to [Xidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xidan), where "65 PLA trucks and 47 APCs ... were totally destroyed, and 485 other military vehicles were damaged."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaum1996283-50] Although troops advanced into Beijing from all directions, the majority of deaths during the night of 3 June occurred around the Muxidi area.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001203-201][^cite_note-The_Daily_Telegraph-202][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERichelsonEvans1999b-203][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETimothy_Brook_interview2006-190][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartel2006-204][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a38-205]

Throughout the street fighting, demonstrators attacked troops with poles, rocks, and [molotov cocktails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov_cocktails); [Jeff Widener](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Widener) reported witnessing rioters setting fire to military vehicles and beating the soldiers inside them to death.[^cite_note-jeff-206] On one avenue in western Beijing, anti-government protestors torched a military convoy of more than 100 trucks and armoured vehicles.[^cite_note-207] They also hijacked an armoured personnel carrier, taking it on a joy ride. These scenes were captured on camera and broadcast by Chinese state television.[^cite_note-kristof-208]

In the evening, a firefight broke out between soldiers and demonstrators at Shuangjing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002509-209]

On 5 June 1989, *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported on the fighting: "As columns of tanks and tens of thousands of soldiers approached Tiananmen, many troops were set on by angry mobs who screamed, 'Fascists'. Dozens of soldiers were pulled from trucks, severely beaten, and left for dead. At an intersection west of the square, the body of a young soldier, who had been beaten to death, was stripped naked and hung from the side of a bus. Another soldier's corpse was strung up at an intersection east of the square."[^cite_note-Class_Struggle:_China's_Harsh_Actions_Threaten_to_Set_Back_10-Year_Reform_Drive-188]

#### Clearing the square

At the Square, soldiers and students initially attempted to show restraint, but residents refused to follow student orders.[^cite_note-embassydoc15-210] Soldiers did not fight back at first after some citizens threw rocks at them.[^cite_note-embassydoc14-211][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002497-212]

In the early morning on 4 June, the first APC entered Tiananmen Square from Chang'an West Boulevard. Demonstrators attacked the APC with molotov cocktails and immobilised it with a traffic divider, before covering it with gasoline-doused blankets and setting it on fire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002497-212] Wu Renhua claims that after the three occupants were forced out of the vehicle by the heat, students escorted the three men to a medic station.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWu:_Last_Act_(1)-213] However, according to an eyewitness account of a Chinese-American reporter, the soldiers were attacked by the crowd. Two soldiers were burned alive inside the APC, and a third was dragged out of the vehicle and beaten to death in full view of other soldiers.[^cite_note-embassydoc14-211][^cite_note-embassydoc15-210] [Larry Wortzel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Wortzel), a military intelligence officer at the U.S. Embassy at the time, noted that the demonstrators' swarming tactics were clearly rehearsed and practiced, having been used similarly in other places around the city.[^cite_note-wortzel-214]

Troops from the west arrived at the square at about 1\:30 am, and troops from other directions gradually arrived as well, blocking main roads to the square to prevent entry.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001205-215] A second emergency announcement from the government was broadcast on loudspeakers:

> A severe counterrevolutionary riot has broken out in the capital tonight. Rioters have savagely attacked soldiers of the PLA, have stolen their weapons and burned their vehicles, have erected roadblocks, and have kidnapped officers and soldiers \[...] Citizens and students must evacuate the Square immediately so that martial law troops can successfully carry out their mission. We cannot guarantee the safety of violators, who will be solely responsible for any consequences.

— Emergency Announcement, Beijing Municipal Government and Martial Law Command[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002498-216]

After the announcement, most people in the square began to leave, and by 2\:00 am, there were only a few thousand demonstrators in the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002498-216] North of the square, a dozen students and citizens attempted to torch army trucks with cans of gasoline but were arrested.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002498-216]

At 3\:00 am, Hou Dejian, Liu Xiaobo, Zhou Duo, and Gao Xin decided to convince the students to evacuate the square; Chai Ling, however, insisted that "those who wish to leave may leave, and those who don't may stay."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002500-217] The group asked Chai Ling and other student leaders to negotiate a peaceful evacuation. Hou Dejian addressed the students by loudspeaker, urging them to leave the square and surrender their rifles and other weapons, before leaving with Zhou Duo in an ambulance to meet the government troops.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001205-215][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002500-217]

Between 3\:30 and 3\:45 am, the ambulance arrived at the Museum of Chinese History in the northeast corner of the square, and Hou Dejian and Zhou Duo met with Ji Xinguo, a regimental political commissar.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001205-215][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002500-217] They requested that the army give them time to evacuate, and to open a path for them to leave. Ji Xinguo relayed their request to Martial Law Headquarters, who agreed to the students' request.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001205-215][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002500-217] Ji Xinguo informed them of this and told them to exit to the south. After Hou and Zhou returned to the square, they called for an immediate evacuation, and the Martial Law Headquarters announced, "Students, we appreciate that you will leave the Square voluntarily. Students, please leave in the southeastern direction."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001206-218]

There was initial reluctance among the students to leave, but as the deadline approached, Feng Congde asked students for a voice vote on whether to stay or leave.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001206-218] Although the vote's results were inconclusive, Feng said the vote to leave was louder.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001206-218] The demonstrators began to evacuate, with students leaving under their school banners, heading southeast.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENathan2002500-217][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohn_Pomfret_interview2006-199] At about 4\:35 am, a few minutes after the demonstrators started to retreat, the lights in the square were turned on, and troops began to advance. A squad of commandos charged up the monument and shot out the students' loudspeaker.[^cite_note-cjr-219][^cite_note-FOOTNOTED._Zhao2001206-218] According to Hou Dejian, tear gas was used during the operation to clear the square.[^cite_note-220]

At 5\:23 am, a Chinese armoured car rammed the Goddess of Democracy statue several times, and then removed its severed torch after it collapsed as a memento.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaum1996288-221]

Having removed the students from the square, soldiers were ordered to relinquish their ammunition, after which they were allowed a short reprieve, from 7 am to 9 am.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a23-222] The debris left over from the student occupation was either piled and burnt on the square or placed in large plastic bags that were then airlifted away by military helicopters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a19-223][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELilley1989-224] After the cleanup, the troops stationed at The Great Hall of the People remained confined within for the next nine days. During this time, the soldiers were apparently left to sleep on the floors and were daily supplied a single packet of instant noodles shared between three men.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a25–26-225]

#### In the city

Just past 6\:00 on 4 June, as a convoy of students who had vacated the square were walking westward in the bicycle lane along Chang'an Avenue back to campus, three tanks pursued them from the square, firing tear gas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoonan2008-226] One tank proceeded to ram the crowd, killing 11 students and injuring scores of others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWu:_Tanks_Ran_Over_Students-227]

By dawn, the sky was enveloped with smoke rising from the centre of the city, and the streets were filled with burned-out vehicles.[^cite_note-wortzel-214] Skirmishes between protesters and troops continued sporadically in the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Gate_of_Heavenly1995-71][^cite_note-228] Some demonstrators regrouped and attempted to re-enter the square from the northeast on East Chang'an Avenue, but were driven off by gunfire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas200632\:23–34\:50-229] The square remained closed to the public for two weeks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchoenberger1989-230]

At 17\:00, a sitrep from the U.S. State Department to the U.S. Embassy indicated that the PLA was "mopping up isolated resistance".[^cite_note-embassydoc16-231]

### 5 June and the Tank Man

Main article: [Tank Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tank_Man)



"Tank Man" temporarily stopping the advance of four [Type 59](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_59_tank) tanks

On June 5, having secured the square, the military began to reassert control over thoroughfares through the city, especially Chang'an Avenue. A column of tanks of the 1st Armored Division left the square and, heading east on Chang'an Avenue, came upon a lone protester standing in the middle of the avenue. The brief standoff between the man and the tanks was captured by Western media atop the Beijing Hotel. After returning to his position in front of the tanks, the man was pulled aside by a group of people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas2006-16] [Charlie Cole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Cole_\(photographer\)), who was there for *[Newsweek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsweek)*, claimed that the men were Chinese [government agents](44458059.md),[^cite_note-BBC_News-232] while [Jan Wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Wong), who was there for *[The Globe and Mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Globe_and_Mail)*, thought that they were concerned bystanders.[^cite_note-wong-233] Contemporary sources and Timothy Brook's analysis identified Tank Man as 19-year-old Wang Weilin, son of a factory worker, who was subsequently sentenced to ten years in prison.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998177-234] Although the fate of Tank Man following the demonstration is not known, paramount Chinese leader Jiang Zemin stated in 1990 that he did not think the man was killed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIyer1998-235] *Time* later named him one of the [100 most influential people of the 20th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100:_The_Most_Important_People_of_the_Century).

A stopped convoy of 37 APCs on Changan Boulevard at Muxidi was forced to abandon their vehicles after becoming stuck among an assortment of burned-out buses and military vehicles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998178–179-236] In addition to occasional incidents of soldiers opening fire on civilians in Beijing, Western news outlets reported clashes between units of the PLA.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristof:_Units_Clash-237] Late in the afternoon, 26 tanks, three armoured personnel carriers, and supporting infantry took up defensive positions facing east at [Jianguomen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jianguomen_\(Beijing\)) and [Fuxingmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuxingmen) overpasses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998189-238] Shellfire was heard throughout the night, and the next morning a United States Marine in the eastern part of the city reported spotting a damaged armoured vehicle that an armour-piercing shell had disabled.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998190-239] The ongoing turmoil in the capital disrupted everyday life flow. No editions of the *People's Daily* were available in Beijing on 5 June, despite assurances that they had been printed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristof:_Units_Clash-237] Many shops, offices, and factories were not able to open, as workers remained in their homes, and public transit services were limited to the subway and suburban bus routes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong1989-240]

By and large, the government regained control in the week following the square's military seizure. A political purge followed in which officials responsible for organizing or condoning the protests were removed, and protest leaders were jailed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsuiPang-241]

### Protests outside Beijing

After order was restored in Beijing on 4 June, protests of various sizes continued in some 80 other Chinese cities outside the international press's spotlight.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAgeing_rebels,_bitter_victims2014-242] In the British colony of Hong Kong, people again took to wearing black in solidarity with the demonstrators in Beijing. There were also protests in other countries, where many adopted the wearing of black armbands as well.[^cite_note-243]

In Shanghai, students marched on the streets on 5 June and erected roadblocks on major thoroughfares. Public transport including railway traffic was blocked.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001530-244] On 6 June, the municipal government tried to clear the rail blockade, but it was met with fierce resistance from the crowds. Several people were killed from being run over by a train.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001530-244] On 7 June, students from major Shanghai universities stormed various campus facilities to erect [biers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bier).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001535-245] Although there were rumours of possible martial law in the city, the situation was gradually brought under control without deadly force.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001535-540-246] The municipal government gained recognition from the top leadership in Beijing for averting a major upheaval.

In the interior cities of Xi'an, Wuhan, Nanjing, and Chengdu, many students continued protests after 4 June, often erecting roadblocks. In Chengdu, [large protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chengdu_protests_of_1989) were violently crushed by security forces on 4 and 5 June, with eyewitnesses reporting "300 to 400 people killed".[^cite_note-247] In Xi'an, students stopped workers from entering factories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001399,_404-248] In Wuhan, students blocked the [Yangtze River Railway bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan_Yangtze_River_Bridge) and another 4,000 gathered at the railway station.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001400-249] About one thousand students staged a railroad "sit-in", and rail traffic on the Beijing-Guangzhou and Wuhan-Dalian lines was interrupted. The students also urged employees of major state-owned enterprises to go on strike.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001405-250] In Wuhan, the situation was so tense that residents reportedly began a [bank run](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_run) and resorted to [panic buying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic_buying).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001408-251]

### Government's pronouncements

See also: [June 9 Deng speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_9_Deng_speech)

At a news conference on 6 June, State Council spokesperson Yuan Mu announced that based on "preliminary statistics", "nearly 300 people died ... includ\[ing] soldiers", 23 students, "bad elements who deserve\[d] this because of their crimes, and people who were killed by mistake."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOksenbergSullivanLambert1990364-252] He said the wounded included "5,000 \[police] officers and \[soldiers]" and over "2,000 civilians, including the handful of lawless ruffians and the onlooking masses who do understand the situation."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOksenbergSullivanLambert1990364-252] Military spokesperson Zhang Gong stated that no one was killed in Tiananmen Square and no one was run over by tanks in the square.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOksenbergSullivanLambert1990361–367-253]

On 9 June, [Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping), appearing in public for the first time since the protests began, delivered a speech praising the "martyrs" (PLA soldiers who had died).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELiLiMark201167-254][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeng_Xiaoping1989-255][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeng's_9_June_Speech1989-256] Deng stated that the goal of the student movement was to overthrow the party and the state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinDeng1989154–158-257] Of the protesters, Deng said: "Their goal is to establish a totally Western-dependent bourgeois republic." Deng argued that protesters had complained about corruption to cover their real motive, replacing the socialist system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOksenbergSullivanLambert1990378-258] He said that "the entire imperialist Western world plans to make all socialist countries discard the socialist road and then bring them under the monopoly of international capital and onto the capitalist road."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFewsmith200142-259]

## Death toll

See also: [People's Liberation Army at the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre § Casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_at_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre#Casualties)

From the end of the events on, the number of people who died in the square itself has been in dispute. The government actively suppressed discussion of casualty figures immediately after the events, and estimates rely heavily on eyewitness testimony, hospital records, and organised efforts by victims' relatives. As a result, large discrepancies exist among various casualty estimates. Initial estimates ranged from the official figure of a few hundred to several thousand.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998151–169-260]

### Official figures

Official government announcements shortly after the event put the number who died at about 300. According to East German Politburo member [Günter Schabowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BCnter_Schabowski), Jiang Zemin told him that "around 400 people" had died during a visit shortly after the massacre.[^cite_note-261] At the State Council press conference on 6 June, spokesman [Yuan Mu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_Mu) said that "preliminary tallies" by the government showed that about 300 civilians and soldiers died, including 23 students from universities in Beijing, along with some people he described as "ruffians".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOksenbergSullivanLambert1990364-252][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998167-262] Yuan also said some 5,000 soldiers and police were wounded, along with 2,000 civilians. On 19 June, Beijing Party Secretary [Li Ximing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Ximing) reported to the Politburo that the government's confirmed death toll was 241, including 218 civilians (of which 36 were students), 10 PLA soldiers, and 13 People's Armed Police, along with 7,000 wounded.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001436-263][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrontline:_Memory_of_Tiananmen2006-264] On 30 June, [Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Beijing) [Chen Xitong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Xitong) said that the number of injured was about 6,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998167-262][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOksenbergSullivanLambert1990364-252]

### Other estimates



A burned-out vehicle on a Beijing street a few days after 6 June

On the morning of 4 June, many estimates of deaths were reported, including from government-affiliated sources. Peking University leaflets circulated on campus suggested a death toll of between two and three thousand. The [Chinese Red Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Cross_Society_of_China) was rumoured to have given a figure of 2,600 deaths, but the organization denied having given such a figure.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHow_Many_Died1990-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Sino-American_relations''1991445-22] The Swiss Ambassador had estimated 2,700, following a visit to Beijing's hospitals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998154-23] Records from Beijing's main eleven hospitals, compiled shortly after the events, recorded at least 478 dead and 920 wounded, though Timothy Brook notes that these figures are an undercount due to lack of information from other hospitals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998161-265] [Jonathan Fenby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Fenby) puts the death toll at over 700 in [his book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Search_for_Modern_China).[^cite_note-266]

[Nicholas D. Kristof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_D._Kristof) of *[The New York Times](30680.md)* wrote on 21 June that "it seems plausible that about a dozen soldiers and policemen were killed, along with 400 to 800 civilians."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristof:_Reassessing_Casualties-24] United States ambassador [James Lilley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Lilley) said that, based on visits to hospitals around Beijing, a minimum of several hundred had been killed.[^cite_note-267] A declassified [National Security Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Agency) cable filed on the same day estimated 180–500 deaths up to the morning of 4 June.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERichelsonEvans1999b-203] [Amnesty International](18947898.md)'s estimates put the number of deaths at between several hundred and close to 1,000,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHow_Many_Died1990-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalls_for_Justice2004-26] while a Western diplomat who compiled estimates put the number at 300 to 1,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristof:_Reassessing_Casualties-24]

In a highly disputed cable sent in 24 hours after the events at Tiananmen, British Ambassador [Alan Donald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Donald) initially claimed, based on information from a "good friend" in the [State Council of China](44498.md), that a minimum of 10,000 civilians died,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELusher2017-268] claims which were repeated in a speech by Australian Prime Minister [Bob Hawke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Hawke),[^cite_note-\:0-269] but which is an estimated number much higher than other sources.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998169-270][^cite_note-271] After the declassification, student protest leader [Feng Congde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feng_Congde) pointed out that Donald later revised his estimate to 2,700–3,400 deaths, a number closer to, but still much higher than, other estimates.[^cite_note-272] [Timothy Brook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timothy_Brook) also finds that claims of 10,000 fatalities are "extravagant" and "unconvincing".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrook1998169-270]

### Identifying the dead

The [Tiananmen Mothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiananmen_Mothers), a victims' advocacy group co-founded by [Ding Zilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ding_Zilin) and [Zhang Xianling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Xianling), whose children were killed by the government during the crackdown, have identified 202 victims as of August 2011.[^cite_note-tiananmenmother-273] In the face of government interference, the group has worked painstakingly to locate victims' families and collect information about the victims. Their tally had grown from 155 in 1999 to 202 in 2011, where it remains in 2026.[^cite_note-tiananmenmother-273] The list includes four individuals who committed suicide on or after 4 June for reasons related to their involvement in the demonstrations.[^cite_note-tiananmenmother-273][^cite_note-274]

### Deaths around and in Tiananmen Square itself

Government officials have long asserted that no one died in the square in the early morning hours of 4 June, during the "hold-out" of students' last batch in the south part of the square. Initially, foreign media reports of a "massacre" on the square were prevalent, though subsequently, journalists have acknowledged that most of the deaths occurred outside of the square in western Beijing. Several people who were situated around the square that night, including former Beijing bureau chief of *The Washington Post* [Jay Mathews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Mathews)[^cite_note-276] and CBS correspondent Richard Roth[^cite_note-278] reported that while they had heard sporadic gunfire, they could not find enough evidence to suggest that a massacre took place on the square.

Student leader [Chai Ling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chai_Ling) claimed in a speech broadcast on Hong Kong television that she witnessed tanks arrive at the square and crush students who were sleeping in their tents, and added that between 200 and 400 students died at the square.[^cite_note-279] Ling was joined by fellow student leader [Wu'er Kaixi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wu'erkaixi) who said he had witnessed 200 students being cut down by gunfire; however, according to Mathews, it was later proven that he had already left the square several hours before the events he claimed to have happened.[^cite_note-cjr-219] Taiwan-born [Hou Dejian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hou_Dejian) was present in the square to show solidarity with the students and said that he did not see any massacre occurring in the square. He was quoted by Xiaoping Li, a former China dissident to have stated: "Some people said 200 died in the square, and others claimed that as many as 2,000 died. There were also stories of tanks running over students who were trying to leave. I have to say I did not see any of that. I was in the square until 6\:30 in the morning."[^cite_note-280]

In 2009, human rights activist [Robin Munro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Munro), stated that he was present during the clearance of Tiananmen Square and that, according to his account, there were ten Western reporters near the Monument to the People's Heroes at the time. He also reported a Spanish television crew that recorded footage of the event. Munro disputed Wu'er Kaixi's claims of mass killings inside the square, stating that he did not witness fighting there and instead observed students peacefully complying with the soldiers' demands to leave the square in an orderly manner. He further noted that the Spanish TV crew reported not seeing killings within the square during the final clearance.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282]

In 2011, three secret cables from the [United States embassy in Beijing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_the_United_States,_Beijing) from the time of the events were leaked and published by [WikiLeaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WikiLeaks), all of which stated that there was no bloodshed inside Tiananmen Square itself.[^cite_note-The_Daily_Telegraph-202] Instead, they said Chinese soldiers opened fire on protesters in Beijing outside the square, around [Muxidi station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muxidi_station), as they fought their way from the west towards the centre.[^cite_note-The_Daily_Telegraph-202] A Chilean diplomat who had been positioned next to a Red Cross station inside the square told his US counterparts that he did not observe any mass firing of weapons into the crowds in the square itself, although sporadic gunfire was heard. He said that most of the troops who entered the square were armed only with anti-riot gear.[^cite_note-The_Daily_Telegraph-202][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELilley1989-224]

## Immediate aftermath

### &#x20;Arrests, punishments, and evacuations

Main articles: [Dissidents in the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissidents_in_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre) and [Operation Yellowbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Yellowbird)

See also: [Women's roles during the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_roles_during_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre)

On 13 June 1989, the [Beijing Public Security Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Public_Security_Bureau) released an order for the arrest of 21 students they identified as the protest leaders. These 21 most-wanted student leaders were part of the [Beijing Students Autonomous Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Students'_Autonomous_Federation),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEE._Cheng2012-283] which had been instrumental in the Tiananmen Square protests. Though decades have passed, this most-wanted list has never been retracted by the Chinese government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a74-284]

On 17 June, two individuals were captured after exchanging gunfire with soldiers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchoenberger1989-230] The same day, a woman who had been visiting a Western diplomat at Jianguomenwai diplomatic compound was observed being arrested by plainclothes policemen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchoenberger1989-230]

The 21 most-wanted student leaders' faces and descriptions were often broadcast on television.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a39-285][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristof:_Moderates_Appear-286] Photographs with biographies of the leaders followed in this order: [Wang Dan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Dan_\(dissident\)), [Wuer Kaixi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wu'erkaixi), [Liu Gang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Gang), [Chai Ling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chai_Ling), [Zhou Fengsuo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Fengsuo), [Zhai Weimin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhai_Weimin?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Liang Qingdun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liang_Qingdun?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Wang Zhengyun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Zhengyun?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Zheng Xuguang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zheng_Xuguang?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Ma Shaofang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Shaofang?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Yang Tao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang_Tao_\(dissident\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Wang Zhixing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Zhixing_\(dissident\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Feng Congde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feng_Congde), [Wang Chaohua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Chaohua), [Wang Youcai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Youcai), [Zhang Zhiqing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Zhiqing_\(dissident\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Zhang Boli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Boli), [Li Lu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Lu), [Zhang Ming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Ming_\(activist\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Xiong Wei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiong_Wei_\(dissident\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), and [Xiong Yan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiong_Yan_\(dissident\)).

Each of the 21 students faced diverse experiences after their arrests or escapes; while some remain abroad with no intent to return, others have chosen to stay indefinitely, such as Zhang Ming.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a70–71-287] Only 7 of the 21 were able to escape.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMosher2004-288] Some student leaders, such as [Chai Ling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chai_Ling) and [Wuer Kaixi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuer_Kaixi), were able to escape to the United States, the United Kingdom, France, and other Western nations under [Operation Yellowbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Yellowbird), which was organised by Western intelligence agencies such as [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6) and [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA) from [Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Hong_Kong), a British territory at the time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELiu1996-289][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a70–71-287][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMosher2004-288][^cite_note-290] According to *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, the operation involved more than 40 people and had its roots in the [Alliance in Support of Democratic Movements in China](1974713.md) formed in May 1989. After the Beijing protest crackdown, this group drew up an initial list of 40 dissidents they believed could form the nucleus of a "Chinese democracy movement in exile".[^cite_note-291]

The remaining student leaders were apprehended and incarcerated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMosher2004-288] Those who escaped, whether in 1989 or after, generally have had difficulty re-entering China up to this day.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014a73–76-292] The Chinese government has preferred to leave the dissidents in exile.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETraywick2013-293] Those who attempt to re-enter, such as Wu'er Kaixi, have been simply sent back but not arrested.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETraywick2013-293]

[Chen Ziming and Wang Juntao were arrested](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrest_and_Trial_of_Chen_Ziming_and_Wang_Juntao) in late 1989 for their involvement in the protests. Chinese authorities alleged they were the "black hands" behind the movement. Both Chen and Wang rejected the allegations made against them. They were put on trial in 1990 and sentenced to 13 years in prison.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeber2014-294] Others, such as Zhang Zhiqing, have essentially disappeared. After his initial arrest in January 1991 and subsequent release, nothing further is known about his situation and where he lives now.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMosher2004-288] Zhang Zhiqing's role and reason for being listed on the 21 most wanted is generally unknown; this is the case for many others on the list, such as Wang Chaohua.

According to the [Dui Hua Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dui_Hua_Foundation?action=edit\&redlink=1), citing a provincial government, 1,602 individuals were imprisoned for protest-related activities in early 1989. As of May 2012, at least two remain incarcerated in Beijing, and five others remain unaccounted for.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELess_Than_a_Dozen2012-295] In June 2014, it was reported that Miao Deshun was believed to be the last known prisoner incarcerated for their participation in the protests; he was last heard from a decade ago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarron2014-296] All are reported to have mental illnesses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELess_Than_a_Dozen2012-295]

### Leadership changes

Main article: [Political purges during and after the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_purges_during_and_after_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre)



[Jiang Zemin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Zemin) (1926–2022), the party secretary of Shanghai, where student protests were subdued largely without violence, was promoted to succeed [Zhao Ziyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhao_Ziyang) as the party [General Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) in 1989.

The party leadership expelled Zhao Ziyang from the [Politburo Standing Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politburo_Standing_Committee) (PSC). [Hu Qili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Qili), another PSC member who opposed martial law but abstained from voting, was also removed from the committee. However, he was able to retain his party membership, and after "changing his opinion", he was reassigned as deputy minister in the Ministry for Machinery and Electronics Industry. Another reform-minded Chinese leader, [Wan Li](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wan_Li), was also put under house arrest immediately after he stepped out of his plane at [Beijing Capital Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Capital_International_Airport) upon returning from a shortened trip abroad; the authorities declared his detention to be on health grounds. When Wan Li was released from his house arrest after he finally "changed his opinion", he, like [Qiao Shi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiao_Shi), was transferred to a different position with equal rank but a mostly ceremonial role. Several Chinese ambassadors abroad claimed political asylum.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBernstein1989b-297]

[Jiang Zemin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Zemin), the Party Secretary of Shanghai, was promoted to [General Secretary of the Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party). Jiang's decisive actions in Shanghai involving the *World Economic Herald* and his having prevented deadly violence in the city won him support from party elders in Beijing. Having put the new leadership team in place and recognizing his weakened position, Deng Xiaoping himself also bowed out of the party leadership—at least officially—by resigning his last leadership position as Chairman of the [Central Military Commission](214345.md) later that year. He kept a low profile until 1992. According to diplomatic cables de-classified by Canada, the Swiss ambassador informed Canadian diplomats in confidence that over several months following the massacre, "every member of the Politburo Standing Committee has approached him about transferring very significant amounts of money to Swiss bank accounts."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips2015-298]

[Bao Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bao_Tong), Zhao Ziyang's aide, was the highest-ranking official to be formally charged with a crime connected with 1989 demonstrations. He was convicted in 1992 of "revealing state secrets and counter-revolutionary propagandizing" and served seven years in prison. To purge sympathizers of Tiananmen demonstrators from among the party's rank-and-file, the party leadership initiated a one-and-a-half-year-long rectification programme to "deal strictly with those inside the party with serious tendencies toward bourgeois liberalisation". Four million people were reportedly investigated for their role in the protests. More than 30,000 Communist officers were deployed to assess the "political reliability" of more than one million government officials.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiles199727–30-299] The authorities arrested tens if not hundreds of thousands of people across the country. Some were seized in broad daylight while they walked in the street; others were arrested at night. Many were jailed or sent to labour camps. They were often denied access to see their families and often put in cells so crowded that not everyone had space to sleep. Dissidents shared cells with murderers and rapists, and torture was not uncommon.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiles199730-300]

### Media coverage

#### Official narrative

The official narrative constructed by the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) on 4 June Incident states that the use of force was necessary to control "political turmoil",[^cite_note-HRW-2009-301] and this also ensures the stable society that is necessary for successful economic development.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304] Chinese leaders—including [Jiang Zemin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Zemin) and [Hu Jintao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Jintao), who were general secretaries of the Central Committee of the Chinese Communist Party—consistently reiterated the official narrative of the Chinese Communist Party when being asked about the protests by foreign journalists.[^cite_note-305]

Print media were required to be consistent with the Chinese government's account of the 4 June Incident.[^cite_note-HRW-2009-301] The Chinese government prepared a [white paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_paper) to explain the government's views on the protests. Later, anonymous people within the Chinese government shipped the files overseas and published the "[Tiananmen Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tiananmen_Papers)" in 2001. At the 30th anniversary of the 4 June Incident, [Wei Fenghe](38755987.md), a general of the Chinese People's Liberation Army, said in the [Shangri-La Dialogue](5410193.md): "The 4 June Incident was a turmoil and unrest. The Central Government took decisive measures to calm the unrest and stop the turmoil, and it is because of this decision that the stability within the country can be established. For the past three decades, China has undergone tremendous changes under the leadership of the Communist Party."[^cite_note-306]

#### Chinese media

See also: [People's Daily during the 1989 Student Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Daily_during_the_1989_Student_Movement)

The suppression of 4 June marked the end of a period of relative press freedom in China, and media workers—both foreign and domestic—faced heightened restrictions and punishment in the aftermath of the crackdown. State media reports in the immediate aftermath were sympathetic to the students. As a result, those responsible were all later removed from their posts. Two news anchors, [Du Xian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Du_Xian_\(news_anchor\)) and [Xue Fei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xue_Fei_\(host\)), who reported this event on 4 and 5 June, respectively in the daily *[Xinwen Lianbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinwen_Lianbo)* broadcast on [China Central Television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Central_Television), were fired because they openly emoted in sympathy with the protesters. Wu Xiaoyong, the son of the former foreign minister [Wu Xueqian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wu_Xueqian), was removed from the English Programme Department of [Chinese Radio International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Radio_International), ostensibly for his sympathies towards protesters. Editors and other staff at the *[People's Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Daily)*, including director [Qian Liren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qian_Liren) and Editor-in-Chief Tan Wenrui, were also sacked because of reports in the paper that were sympathetic towards the protesters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWudunn1989-307]

The restrictions were loosened after a few years had passed, especially after [Deng Xiaoping's southern tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping's_southern_tour).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabestan2010195-308] Privately run print media then again flourished. Private newspapers increased from 250 in the 1980s to over 7,000 by 2003. Provincially-run, satellite TV stations sprang up all over the country and challenged the market share of state-run CCTV.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurlantzick200350-309] The leadership also stepped away from promoting communism as an all-encompassing belief system. State-approved religious organisations increased their membership significantly, and traditional beliefs suppressed during the Mao era re-appeared.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurlantzick200350-309] This state-sanctioned plurality also created an environment for unsanctioned forms of spirituality and worship to grow.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurlantzick200352-310] To reduce the need for controversial methods of state control, Protestants, Buddhists, and Taoists were often used by the state as "approved" denominations to "fight against cults" such as [Falun Gong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falun_Gong), playing the sects against each other.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurlantzick200352-310]

#### Foreign media

Main article: [Foreign media at the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_media_at_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre)

With the imposition of martial law, the Chinese government cut off Western broadcasters' satellite transmissions such as CNN and CBS. Broadcasters tried to defy these orders by reporting via telephone. Video footage was smuggled out of the country, although the only network that was able to record video during the night of 4 June was [Televisión Española](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Televisi%C3%B3n_Espa%C3%B1ola) of Spain (TVE).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBregolat2007-311] During the military action, some foreign journalists faced harassment from authorities. [CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS) correspondent [Richard Roth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Roth_\(journalist\)) and his cameraman were taken into custody while filing a report from the square via mobile phone.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoth2009a-312]

Several foreign journalists who had covered the crackdown were expelled in the weeks that followed, while others were harassed by authorities or blacklisted from reentering the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristof:_Beijing_Ousts_Correspondents-313] Some were reportedly beaten.[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315]

Some Western outlets withheld certain images from viewers;[^cite_note-jptimes-316] Graham Earnshaw, a [Reuters](18998750.md) correspondent at the time, stated that a "disgusting" photo depicting the burned body of a soldier hanging from a bus was not sent to subscribers.[^cite_note-earnshaw-317]

## Reactions

Main article: [Reactions to the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactions_to_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre)



[Monument in Memory of Chinese from Tiananmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_in_Memory_of_Chinese_from_Tiananmen) in [Wrocław](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wroc%C5%82aw), Poland

The Chinese government's response was widely denounced by Western governments and media.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETiananmen_Square_Document_351989-318]

According to [Suisheng Zhao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suisheng_Zhao), Asian countries generally remained silent throughout the protests.[^cite_note-Zhao-2023-319] India's government under Prime Minister [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi) ordered state television to minimize coverage of the events in Beijing so as not to jeopardize a thawing in relations with China and to empathize with the Chinese government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMohan2009-320] The [Communist Party of India (Marxist)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_India_\(Marxist\)) was the only political party in the world to pass a resolution supporting the crackdown on the Tiananmen Square protests, calling them "an imperialist attempt to internally subvert socialism, \[which] was successfully thwarted by the CPC and the PLA."[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-Tharoor_2013-322][^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324] South Korea was in the midst of efforts to develop relations with the People's Republic of China and President [Roh Tae-woo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roh_Tae-woo) remained quiet in the aftermath of the crackdown.[^cite_note-325]<sup>: 166 </sup>

Former United Nations Secretary-General [Javier Pérez de Cuéllar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javier_P%C3%A9rez_de_Cu%C3%A9llar) expressed great sadness over the event.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETiananmen_Square_Document_35198911-326] In 1992, he however stated that the crackdown had been exaggerated, and that he received no convincing evidence of widespread killing.[^cite_note-independent-327]

Cuba, Czechoslovakia, and East Germany, under the Soviet sphere of influence at the time, supported the Chinese government and denounced the protests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETiananmen_Square_Document_351989-318]

Amnesty International condemned the prosecution of Hong Kong activists Chow Hang-tung and Lee Cheuk-yan, arguing that it is part of a broader strategy to use national security laws to suppress dissent and erase historical memory of the 1989 Tiananmen Square crackdown. Both activists, former members of the disbanded Hong Kong Alliance, have been detained for over four years and face lengthy imprisonment for advocating justice for the victims. Amnesty calls for their immediate release, the repeal of the National Security Law, and the protection of the right to peacefully commemorate the Tiananmen massacre.[^cite_note-328]

## Longer-term impact

### Politics

Further information: [Political purges during and after the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_purges_during_and_after_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre)

As the CCP departed from the orthodox communism it was founded upon, much of its attention was focused on the cultivation of nationalism as an alternative ideology.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabestan2010199-329] This policy largely succeeded in tying the party's legitimacy to China's "national pride", turning domestic public opinion back in its favour.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabestan2010201-330]

Support of liberal reform and liberal democracy among intellectuals decreased, and support for conservative cultural politics increased.[^cite_note-Tu-2025-331]<sup>: 166–167 </sup>

The protests highlighted severe inadequacies in [riot-control equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riot_control#Equipment) and training among both the Chinese military and police.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacKinnon1999-332] The PLA was not trained or equipped to handle riots, and the few PAP riot-control units that were dispatched were insufficient in crowd control.[^cite_note-Scobell2-333] Since 1989, efforts have been made to create effective riot-control units in Chinese cities increased spending on internal security and to an expanded role for the People's Armed Police in handling protests.[^cite_note-Scobell2-333]

### Economy

See also: [Deng Xiaoping's southern tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping's_southern_tour)

After the protests and massacre, many business analysts downgraded their outlook for China's economic future.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaughton2007-54] The violent response to the protests was one of the factors that led to a delay in China's acceptance in the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization), which was not completed until twelve years later, in 2001.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaughton2007-54] Furthermore, bilateral aid to China decreased from \$3.4 billion in 1988 to \$700 million in 1990.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoot2000-334] Loans to China were suspended by the [World Bank](36752.md), [Asian Development Bank](2512.md), and foreign governments;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThakurBurtonSrivastava1997404–405-335] China's credit rating was lowered;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoot2000-334] tourism revenue decreased from US\$2.2 billion to US\$1.8 billion; and foreign direct investment commitments were cancelled. However, there was a rise in government defence spending from 8.6% in 1986, to 15.5% in 1990, reversing a previous 10-year decline.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelleyShenkar1993120–122-336]

Following the protests and massacre, the state paused various marketization reforms or rolled them back in some policy areas.[^cite_note-\:12322232-337]<sup>: 249 </sup> The government sought again to centralize control over the economy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaughton2010-338] though the changes were short-lived. Sensing that conservative policies had again taken a foothold within the party, Deng, now retired from all of his official positions, launched his southern tour in 1992, visiting various cities in the country's most prosperous regions while advocating for further economic reforms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWu2015-34] Partly in response to Deng, by the mid-1990s the country was again pursuing market liberalisation on a scale even greater than those seen in the initial stages of the reforms in the 1980s. Although political liberals were purged from within the party, many of those who were economically liberal remained.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaughton2010-338] The economic shocks caused by the events of 1989, in retrospect, had only a minor and temporary effect on China's economic growth. Indeed, with many previously aggrieved groups now regarding political liberalisation as a lost cause, more of their energy was spent on economic activities. The economy would quickly regain momentum into the 1990s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaughton2010-338]

### Hong Kong



[Candlelight vigil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candlelight_vigil) in Hong Kong in 2009 on the 20th anniversary of the June 4 incident

In [Hong Kong](13404.md), the Tiananmen Square protests led to fears that China would renege on its commitments under [one country, two systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_country,_two_systems), following the impending handover of Hong Kong from the United Kingdom in 1997. In response, Governor [Chris Patten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Patten) tried to expand the [franchise for the Legislative Council of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Hong_Kong_electoral_reform), which led to friction with Beijing. For many [Hongkongers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hongkongers), Tiananmen served as the turning point when they lost trust in the Beijing government. The event, coupled with general uncertainty about the status of Hong Kong after the transfer of sovereignty, led to [a sizable exodus of Hong Kongers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waves_of_mass_migrations_from_Hong_Kong) to Western countries such as Canada and Australia before 1997.

There have been [large candlelight vigils attended by tens of thousands in Hong Kong every year since 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorials_for_the_Tiananmen_Square_protests_of_1989), even after the transfer of power to China in 1997. Despite that, the [June 4th Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_4th_Museum) closed in July 2016, after only two years in its location. The group that runs the museum, the [Hong Kong Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Alliance_in_Support_of_Patriotic_Democratic_Movements_in_China), has started to crowdfund money to open the museum in a new location.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKam2016-339] A virtual version of the museum released online in August 2021 has also been blocked by Chinese telecom companies.[^cite_note-340]



*[Pillar of Shame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pillar_of_Shame)* in the University of Hong Kong in 2020

The events continue to strongly impact perceptions of China, its government, attitudes towards democracy, and the extent to which Hong Kongers should identify as "Chinese". The events of 4 June are seen as representative of the Chinese brand of authoritarianism, and they are often invoked by pro-democracy politicians in Hong Kong, especially in relation to democratic reform in Hong Kong and the territory's relationship with Beijing. Academic studies indicate that those who supported the Tiananmen Square movement's rehabilitation had a tendency to support [democratisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratization) in the territory and the election of pro-democracy parties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2012152,_155-341]

In memory of the events among other monuments at 1997 *[Pillar of Shame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pillar_of_Shame)* with height of 8 metres (26 ft) performed by sculptor [Jens Galschiøt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jens_Galschi%C3%B8t) was placed in [the University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Hong_Kong). On 22 December 2021, it was removed by the university authorities,[^cite_note-342] a move that was condemned by [Wang Dan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Dan_\(dissident\)).[^cite_note-343]

### China's image internationally

The Chinese government drew widespread condemnation for its suppression of the protests. In the immediate aftermath, China seemed to be becoming a [pariah state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pariah_state), increasingly isolated internationally. This was a significant setback for the leadership, who had courted international investment for much of the 1980s, as the country emerged from the chaos of the [Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution); however, [Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping) and the core leadership vowed to continue economic liberalisation policies after 1989.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEL._Zhang2001424-344] From there on, China would work domestically and internationally to reshape its national image from that of a repressive regime to that of a benign global economic and military partner.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabestan2010194-345]

Although the crackdown hurt relations with Western countries, it had relatively little impact on China's relations with its Asian neighbours.[^cite_note-Zhao-2023-319] Professor [Suisheng Zhao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suisheng_Zhao), Director of the Centre for China-US Cooperation at the [University of Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Denver), attributes the slight impact to the fact that "the human rights records in most of these countries were not better than China's. To a certain extent, they were sympathetic to China's struggle against pressures from Western countries."[^cite_note-Zhao-2023-319] Even in the wake of the crackdown, China's foreign relations with its neighbours generally improved.[^cite_note-Zhao-2023-319]



Protest in Hong Kong, 2020

The government signed the [Non-Proliferation Treaty](22107.md) in 1992, the [Convention on Chemical Weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_Weapons_Convention) in 1993, and the [Comprehensive Test Ban Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Nuclear-Test-Ban_Treaty) in 1996.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELink201019-346][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabestan2010199-329] Whereas China had been a member of only 30 international organisations in 1986, it was a member of over 50 by 1997.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabestan2010198-347] China also sought to diversify its external partnerships, establishing good diplomatic relations with post-Soviet Russia,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabestan2010196-348] and welcoming Taiwanese business in lieu of Western investment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabestan2010196-348] China expedited negotiations with the [World Trade Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) and established relations with Indonesia, Israel, South Korea, and others in 1992.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabestan2010199-329]

Furthermore, the government has successfully promoted China as an attractive destination for investment by emphasizing its skilled workers, comparatively low wages, established infrastructure, and sizable consumer base.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurlantzick200355-349] At the same time, the explosion of commercial interest in the country opened the way for multinational corporations to turn a blind eye to politics and human rights in favour of focusing on business interests. Since then, Western leaders who were previously critical of China have sometimes paid lip service to the legacy of Tiananmen in bilateral meetings, but the substance of discussions revolved around business and trade interests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurlantzick200356-350]

### European Union and the United States arms embargo

Main article: [European Union arms embargo on the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_arms_embargo_on_the_People's_Republic_of_China)

The European Union and United States embargo on armament sales to China, put in place due to the violent suppression of the Tiananmen Square protests, remains in place today. China has been calling for a lift of the ban for years and has had a varying amount of support from European Union members. Since 2004, China has portrayed the ban as "outdated" and damaging to China–European Union relations. In early 2004, French President [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac) spearheaded a movement within the European Union to lift the ban, his efforts being supported by German Chancellor [Gerhard Schröder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerhard_Schr%C3%B6der). However, the passing of the [Anti-Secession Law of the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Secession_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China), in March 2005, increased tensions between mainland China and Taiwan, damaging attempts to lift the ban; and several European Union Council members retracted their support for a lift of the ban. Moreover, [Schröder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerhard_Schr%C3%B6der)'s successor [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel) opposed lifting the ban. Members of the United States Congress had also proposed restrictions on the transfer of military technology to the European Union if the latter lifted the ban. The United Kingdom also opposed the embargo lifting when it took charge of the European Union presidency in July 2005.

The [European Parliament](9581.md) has consistently opposed the lifting of the arms embargo to China. Though its agreement is not necessary for lifting the ban, many argue it reflects the will of the European people better as it is the only directly elected European body. The arms embargo has limited China's options in seeking military hardware. Among the sources that were sought included the former [Soviet bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_bloc) that it had a strained relationship with as a result of the [Sino-Soviet split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split). Other willing suppliers have previously included [Israel](9282173.md) and [South Africa](17416221.md), but American pressure has restricted this co-operation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJapan_Concerned-351]

### Chinese disapora

Post-Tiananmen immigration responses in several countries contributed to increased long-term settlement of Chinese nationals already abroad. Following the protests and crackdown, a number of countries including European countries and the United States authorized shortcut avenues for Chinese nationals to get [permanent resident status](1284324.md), affecting tens of thousands of people.[^cite_note-352]

In the United States on 6th June, 1989, President George H. W. Bush stated that he ordered protections for Chinese nationals with [Executive order 12711](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_12711), which suspended deportations for Chinese nationals already in the country. The U.S. government later gave them authorization to work legally in the country. Three years later, the [Chinese Student Protection Act (1992)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Student_Protection_Act_of_1992) was enacted that enabled around 54,000 Chinese nationals to gain permanent residency in the United States.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] A 2012 study published in the *[International Migration Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Migration_Review)*, found that Chinese migrants who had received legal work status and green cards due to the post-Tiananmen policies, performed significantly better in employment and earnings during the 1990s than comparable Asian immigrant groups who did not receive such protections.[^cite_note-357]

On June 9, 1989, the Australian prime minister [Bob Hawke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Hawke) made an infamous televised address where he told the public that soldiers had charged into Tiananmen Square, shooting and bayoneting protesters and that they "had orders that nobody in the square be spared, and children and young girls were slaughtered".[^cite_note-358][^cite_note-359] Although the retelling of events at the square was later found to be inaccurate, Hawke shed tears during his speech and announced that he would offer asylum to approximately 42,000 Chinese nationals already in Australia as a direct consequence.[^cite_note-\:0-269] Hawke in 2015, told *The Guardian* that he acted unilaterally and did not consult anyone on his decision and had a "deep love for the Chinese people".[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361]

In 1990, Chinese immigration to [Vancouver](32706.md) had doubled and in 1994 alone, the city welcomed in 43,000 Chinese immigrants. According to *Legion magazine*, the new demographic was largely professional and educated people.[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363]

## Contemporary issues

### Censorship in China

Further information: [Censorship in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_China)

See also: [Internet censorship of the protest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_censorship_in_China#Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre) and [Chinese censorship abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_censorship_abroad)

The [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) initially responded with a propaganda campaign. The CCP's top leadership decided not to hold a celebration on the one-year anniversary on 4 June 1990. The last 4 June related state publication was published in 1991. Since then, the CCP has imposed a near-total blackout on the events, with even previously government-produced documentaries on the events being banned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChen202247-364]

The Chinese government forbids discussions about the Tiananmen Square protests[^cite_note-365] and has taken measures to block or censor related information, in an attempt to suppress the public's memory of the protests.[^cite_note-China_Erase_History_–_LA_Times_JUNE_24,_2021-47] Textbooks contain little, if any, information about the protests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOlesen2009-366] After the protests, officials banned controversial films and books and[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaiget2009-367] shut down many newspapers. Within a year, 12% of all newspapers, 8% of all publishing companies, 13% of all social science periodicals, and more than 150 films were either banned or shut down. The government also announced that it had seized 32 million contraband books and 2.4 million video and audio cassettes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPei1994152-368] Access to media and Internet resources about the subject are either restricted or blocked by censors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZetter2009-369] Banned literature and films include *[Summer Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summer_Palace_\(2006_film\))*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHiggins2006-370] *[Forbidden City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbidden_City_\(novel\))*, *[The Critical Moment: Li Peng Diaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Critical_Moment)* and any writings of [Zhao Ziyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhao_Ziyang) or his aide [Bao Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bao_Tong), including [Zhao's memoirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoner_of_the_State). The CCP forbids discussing the events even from a pro-government perspective; *[Flying the Flag of the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_the_Flag_of_the_Republic)*, a documentary produced by the PLA after the events that recounts the events from the perspective of the CCP, was eventually banned due to its mentioning of the events,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChen202249-371] while mentions of the [Guardian of the Capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardian_of_the_Capital), awarded to troops who took part in the PLA response, is forbidden from the Chinese internet.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] Due to the extensive censorship around the events, most young people in China are unaware of the Tiananmen Square protests and massacre.[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]

Print media that contain references to the protests must be consistent with the government's version of events.[^cite_note-HRW-2009-301] Domestic and foreign journalists are detained, harassed, or threatened, as are their Chinese colleagues and any Chinese citizens who they interview.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenslade2014-377] Thus, Chinese citizens are typically reluctant to speak about the protests because of potentially negative repercussions. Many young people who were born after 1980 are unfamiliar with the events and are therefore apathetic about politics. Youth in China are generally unaware of the events, the symbols which are associated with them such as the [Tank Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tank_Man),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFisher2014-378][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEApathy-379] or the significance of the date of the massacre 4 June itself.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung_clerk_let2007-380] Some older intellectuals no longer aspire to implement political change. Instead, they focus on economic issues.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGifford2007167–168-381] Some political prisoners have refused to talk to their children about their involvement in the protests out of fear of putting them at risk.[^cite_note-382]

While public discussions about the events have become socially taboo, private discussions about them continue to occur despite frequent interference and harassment by the authorities. Nobel Peace Prize laureate [Liu Xiaobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Xiaobo) remained in China in order to speak out about Tiananmen in the 1990s despite the fact that he received offers of asylum; he faced constant surveillance. [Zhang Xianling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Xianling) and [Ding Zilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ding_Zilin), the mothers of victims who died in 1989, founded the [Tiananmen Mothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiananmen_Mothers) organisation and were particularly outspoken about the humanitarian aspects of the protests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReferences_Censored2009-383] The authorities mobilize security forces, including members of the [People's Armed Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Armed_Police), every year on 4 June in order to prevent public displays of remembrance, with an especially heavy security presence on the anniversaries of major events such as the [20th anniversary of the protests in 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_anniversary_of_the_1989_Tiananmen_Square_protests_and_massacre) and the 25th anniversary of the protests in 2014.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBristow2009-384] On the 30th anniversary of the protests in 2019, the well-known Chinese artist [Ai Weiwei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ai_Weiwei) wrote that "[autocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocracy) and [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism) regimes fear facts because they have built their power on unjust foundations" and he also wrote that memory is important: "without it there is no such thing as a civilised society or nation" because "our past is all we have."[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386]

Journalists have frequently been denied entry to the square on the anniversaries of the massacre.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBristow2009-384][^cite_note-387] Also, the authorities are known to have detained foreign journalists and increase surveillance of prominent human rights activists during this time of year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong2009-388] Internet searches on "4 June Tiananmen Square" made within China return censored results or result in temporarily severed server connections.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReferences_Censored2009-383] Specific web pages with select keywords are censored while other websites, such as those which support the overseas Chinese democracy movement, are blocked wholesale.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOlesen2009-366][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReferences_Censored2009-383] The policy is much more stringent with regard to Chinese-language sites than it is with regard to foreign-language ones. Social media censorship is more stringent in the weeks leading up to the anniversaries of the massacre; even oblique references to the protests and seemingly unrelated terms are usually very aggressively patrolled and censored.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuang2016-389] In January 2006, [Google agreed to censor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_by_Google) their mainland China site to remove information about Tiananmen and other subjects which are considered sensitive by the authorities. Google withdrew its cooperation on censorship in January 2010.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWines2010-390]

In August 2026, *[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde)* published previously unseen footage recorded in 1989 by Nathalie Trouveroy, who said the material was released to help preserve the memory of the victims of the repression.[^cite_note-391] Earlier, in June 2026, more than 2,000 previously unseen photographs of the 1989 Tiananmen protests and crackdown, taken by a Chinese state-media photographer and entrusted to *[The Epoch Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Epoch_Times)*, were publicly released for the first time.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393]

### Calls for the government to reassess

Zhang Shijun, a former soldier who was involved in the military crackdown, published an open letter to President Hu Jintao that sought to have the government reevaluate its position on the protests. He was subsequently arrested.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBranigan2009-394]

Although the Chinese government never officially acknowledged relevant accusations when it came to the incident, in 2005 a payment was made to one of the victims' mothers, the first publicised case of the government offering redress to a Tiananmen-related victim's family. The payment was termed a "hardship assistance" and was given to Tang Deying (唐德英), whose son Zhou Guocong ([simplified Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters): 周国聪; [traditional Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 周國聰) died at age 15 while in police custody in [Chengdu](170375.md) on 6 June 1989, two days after the Chinese Army dispersed the Tiananmen protesters. She was reportedly paid CNY70,000 (approximately US\$10,250).[^cite_note-395] This has been welcomed by various Chinese activists.

### Chinese leaders voicing regret

Before his death in 1998, Yang Shangkun told army doctor [Jiang Yanyong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Yanyong) that 4 June was the most serious mistake committed by the Communist Party in its history, a mistake that Yang himself could not correct, but one that certainly will eventually be corrected.[^cite_note-396] Zhao Ziyang remained under house arrest until his death in 2005. Zhao's aide [Bao Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bao_Tong) has repeatedly called on the government to reverse its verdict on the demonstrations. Chen Xitong, the mayor of Beijing, who read the martial law order and was later disgraced by a political scandal, expressed regret in 2012, a year before his death, for the death of innocent civilians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuckley2013-397] Premier [Wen Jiabao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wen_Jiabao) reportedly suggested reversing the government's position on Tiananmen in party meetings before he departed from politics in 2013, only to be rebuffed by his colleagues.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderlini2013-398]

### United Nations report

In 2008, the [UN Committee Against Torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Committee_Against_Torture) expressed concern over the lack of investigations into the reports of people "killed, arrested or disappeared on or following the 4 June 1989 Beijing suppression." It stated that the Chinese government had also failed to inform relatives of those individuals' fate, despite relatives' numerous requests. Meanwhile, those responsible for the use of excessive force had not "faced any sanction, administrative or criminal."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommittee_Against_Torture20088-399] The Committee recommended that the Chinese government should take all of those steps, plus "offer apologies and reparation as appropriate and prosecute those found responsible for excessive use of force, torture and other ill-treatment."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommittee_Against_Torture20088-399]

In December 2009, the Chinese government responded to the committee's recommendations by saying that the government had closed the case concerning the "political turmoil in the spring and summer of 1989".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommittee_Against_Torture200813-400] It also stated that the "practice of the past 20 years has made it clear that the timely and decisive measures taken by the Chinese Government at the time were necessary and correct". It said that the labelling of the "incident as 'the Democracy Movement'" is a "distortion in the nature of the incident". According to the Chinese government, such observations were "inconsistent with the Committee's responsibilities".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommittee_Against_Torture200813-400]

### Software and IT remembrance

Many Chinese origin techies continue to remember and commemorate Tiananmen event in their own field from time to time. For example, [Notepad++](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notepad++) developer Don Ho released a commemorative version called "Notepad++ v8.9 . 6.4 Tiananmen Massacre Commemoration".[^cite_note-NotepadPlusPlusDownloads-401]

[^cite_note-113]: &#x20; Analyst [Richard Baum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Baum) described their actions as "...a mock-ceremonial remonstrance... presenting their scrolled-up demands on hands and knees in the stylized, obsequious manner of an imperial petition."<sup>[\[105\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaum1996248-111)</sup> Political scientist [Lucian Pye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucian_Pye) similarly described the act as "...in line with the classic Chinese tradition of aggrieved parties wailing before the Yamen door, of publicly dramatizing their unhappiness by petitioning officialdom... \[they] sincerely believed that the officials would have to respond by meeting with them."<sup>[\[106\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPye1990337-112)</sup>&#x20;

[^cite_note-274]: &#x20;Zou Bing (邹冰; victim No. 51), a 19-year-old student at the [Beijing Broadcasting Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Broadcasting_Institute), hanged herself in September 1989 because of her inability to endure interrogation into her involvement in the protest;Ren Jianmin (任建民; victim No. 106) was a farmer from Hebei who was passing through the city and wounded by gunfire in the stomach on 4 June. He was unable to afford medical bills and hanged himself in August because of unbearable pain;Qi Li (齐力; victim No. 162), a student at the [Central Academy of Drama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Academy_of_Drama), hanged himself for escaping the pressure of post-protest investigations at his school;Wei Wumin (韦武民; victim No. 163), also a student at the Central Academy of Drama, who participated in the hunger strike, committed suicide by standing in front of an oncoming train.

[^cite_note-276]: &#x20;Jay Mathews, former Beijing bureau chief for *The Washington Post* said that "as far as can be determined from the available evidence, no one died that night in Tiananmen Square". He goes on to conclude: A few people may have been killed by random shooting on streets near the square, but all verified eyewitness accounts say that the students who remained in the square when troops arrived were allowed to leave peacefully. Hundreds of people, most of them workers and passersby, did die that night, but in a different place and under other circumstances.<sup>[\[267\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMathews1998-275)</sup>

[^cite_note-278]: &#x20;Richard Roth reported that he was held captive by troops in the Great Hall of the People on the west side of the square on the night of 3 June and could hear but not see into the square until dawn when they were driven through the square. He heard a "volley of gunfire" to silence the students' loudspeakers. He added that there is no doubt that many people were killed in the area on the way to and around the square, mostly in western Beijing, which the Communist Party denies.<sup>[\[268\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoth2009b-277)</sup>
